2026-08-29 · Two games in the arcade, and what each one is for

Two games are live. They are free, they run offline once opened, there is no account, no ads, and nothing about how you play leaves your phone.

Peace-Battle (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/offline-os/apps/peace-battle/) · Peace-Battle 2 (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/offline-os/apps/peace-battle-2/) · the arcade (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/arcade/)

Peace-Battle — two rates from 1903

W. E. B. Du Bois, "The Talented Tenth", in The Negro Problem, 1903. The full book is public domain at https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/15041 and is committed alongside the game so the figures can be checked.

He is not proposing a plan. He is describing what the college founders had already done after the war, and he is emphatic about where they started, against the people telling him they should have started lower:

Where ought they to have begun to build? At the bottom, of course, quibbles the mole with his eyes in the earth. Aye! truly at the bottom, at the very bottom; at the bottom of knowledge, down in the very depths of knowledge there where the roots of justice strike into the lowest soil of Truth. And so they did begin; they founded colleges, and up from the colleges shot normal schools, and out from the normal schools went teachers, and around the normal teachers clustered other teachers to teach the public schools; the college trained in Greek and Latin and mathematics, 2,000 men; and these men trained full 50,000 others in morals and manners, and they in turn taught thrift and the alphabet to nine millions of men, who to-day hold $300,000,000 of property.

25 times over, then 180 times over. Those two rates are the only numbers the game runs on.

He called it "the most wonderful peace-battle of the 19th century." That is where the game's name comes from.

Why this sits next to Skills Economy

The starting points are opposite, and the difference is the part worth being precise about.

Du Bois was writing about a population denied education outright. The deficit was skills, and the answer was to build them — colleges first, then teachers out of the colleges, then teachers around those.

Skills Economy starts from the other end. The people here are already highly skilled. What is missing is discovery. Somebody with twenty years in a trade is not short of the trade; they are short of anybody knowing they have it. That is a different problem and it needs a different answer.

The answer is a list. The Directory is a running list of Targeted Individuals alongside what they can do, one person at a time. It is the thing survivors ask for most. Being on it costs nobody an account: most entries were built for people rather than by them, and anybody can claim theirs or have it taken down. Seeing it takes a sign-in, because everything in it came from public sources but gathered and sorted this way it becomes something else.

What the two share is what produced the deficit and what it cost. Both populations face outsized oppression, and in both the result was destitution — reached by different routes, on different spectrums, destitution either way.

And the remedy has the same shape: a connected network of trading. In his it was the colleges, then the teachers those colleges trained, then the nine million those teachers reached — holding $300 million of property by the time he wrote it down. In this one it is people being findable and exchanging services directly with each other.

That is also why there are two games rather than one. The first is his problem — building skills where they were denied. The second is this one — finding the people who already have them.

We have the education. The children will not.

What he described ran from the founding of those colleges after the war to his writing it down in 1903 — about one generation. This could too, which is what the arithmetic actually says but I hold a two-generation goal (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-two-generation-goal) rather than a shorter one.

The math is not what stops it. Depression is. Being targeted causes it, and it makes a number like 25 times over impossible to look at. Freedom is closer than people think.

Adults being targeted now have the education. Children being targeted now will not. That is what this does to a childhood, and denied education is where Du Bois started — a harder place than where we are.

So we hand it down now.

Playing Peace-Battle

You decide each round how much goes to teaching and how much to the work itself. Put everything into the work and you reach 360,000 people. Teach first and you reach 9,000,000 — same effort, same rounds, 25 times the reach. That second split is the one Du Bois recorded, and the game says so when you land on it.

They are his figures, written in 1903 to make an argument rather than to pass an audit. He was not describing a hypothetical; he was describing what a small number of trained people had already done.

There is a preset for where Skills Economy actually stands. As of August 28 it reads 101 people recruited, covering 160 of 657 skills. The rates do not change — only the number it starts from, 101 against his 2,000.

Nobody argues with the arithmetic once they have done it themselves.

Peace-Battle 2 — a map, and nobody wins alone

The second one is a knock-off of Pandemic, a board game from 2008, with the polarity turned around.

In Pandemic a disease spreads across a map of cities and you race to contain it. Here what spreads is isolation — a place where somebody needs something and nobody findable can do it. Four people with skills move against it. People are never the contagion. Being cut off is.

Sixteen real cities, sized by population. Four actions a round: move along a link, or help where you stand. Helping where the need matches your skill does twice as much, and clearing a place puts it in the Directory — the real one, borrowed for the board — covered from then on, its people counted, isolation never returning to it. Then it grows in two more places, and one already at its limit cuts off and pushes into every place beside it. Six cut off and it is over.

Cover the whole map and every place on it is reachable. It is cooperative. There is no winner, and nobody gets there by beating anyone.

That is the difference between the two games. The first shows what a decision about where to put effort is worth. The second makes you produce the outcome where everybody is reachable, and it needs the same thing the real version needs: somebody has to be findable before they can be asked.

Where to find it in the app

- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/two-games-in-the-arcade

2026-08-28 · An Invitation to J.H.B.

The last post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/you-cannot-argue-a-smear-down) answered a question about false narratives. J H B (https://www.quora.com/profile/J-H-B-7) asked it. This is an invitation, and it starts with what they already do, because that makes the argument better than I can.

What they can do

The Directory holds nine specializations, and they are broader than the question they asked would suggest.

Chemical dosing and safety. Diagnostic testing — microscopy, biochemistry, hematology. Pharmacology and prescription. Then a second group with nothing to do with medicine at all: audio recording and mixing, multimedia storytelling, video and audio editing, CCTV, and basic accounting and bookkeeping for small businesses.

[Screenshot — The Directory profile for JHB in the app. It is labeled Community-generated profile, nominated by @farah, located in Australia, with a link out reading View Quora profile — their Quora profile is the social proof, read more before you reach out. Under Specializations: Chemical dosing and safety; Diagnostic testing (microscopy, biochemistry, hematology); Pharmacology and prescription; Prescribing and pharmacology; Audio recording and mixing; Multimedia storytelling (audio/video); Video/audio recording and editing; CCTV; Basic accounting and bookkeeping for SMEs. Below, a panel headed Want to work together? reads: the directory shows who is in the community and what they do. Want a service or good from this person? Look for them in Foundation, where members offer and exchange help.]

That is what the list takes. The Directory records what a person can do, because that is the only part another survivor needs.

And this, which is the line I would have picked if I were writing it for them: you are never alone, just reach out.

Six years of doing it already

Since January 2020: 363 answers, 225 questions, 62 posts, active in ten spaces and running two of them, answering in the spaces where survivors ask whether any of this is real. Read more than four hundred thousand times.

That is not a stated position. That is somebody doing the work, in public, for six years, with their name on it. The distinction worth drawing in this community has never been who holds an account — it is who is doing rather than saying.

Medicine is what we are short of

The app carries a catalog of roughly 650 job titles and skills — the set a working economy of about five million people needs. Every profile is matched against that same list, and only about a quarter of those roles have anyone in them at all (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/your-children-can-have-an-option). The rest of the list is empty rather than competitive.

Medicine is the shortage people feel most. SkillsHunt runs an open mission to find a doctor, on the board because a community this size needs one and does not have one. None of this is being a doctor and I am not going to pretend otherwise. But diagnostic testing is the work that produces the results a doctor reads, and chemical dosing and pharmacology are what stand between a prescription and somebody getting hurt. That is the neighboring ground, and there is nobody standing in it.

Sound is on the list too. So is training somebody else in what you know.

What using it looks like

Not a support group, and not another place to describe the targeting to people who already live it. A Directory profile states what you can do, and other survivors find you by it. Someone needs to know whether two things they were prescribed should be taken together. Someone needs a recording that does not sound like it was made on a phone in a stairwell. Someone is trying to work out where a camera should go and what it will and will not capture.

It is deliberately transactional, and that is the safety mechanism rather than a coldness. Traffickers do not hand people material help — it is the one thing they consistently will not do — so a place organized around giving each other something real filters them out before anyone has to accuse anybody of anything.

The profile is already there

There is a Directory profile for J.H.B. in the app: app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory/profile/2c69e55c-9558-4418-8304-a87590507ee0 (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory/profile/2c69e55c-9558-4418-8304-a87590507ee0)

The app labels it community-generated and names who made it, because I made it rather than its subject. It exists so other survivors can find them by what they can do. J.H.B., it is yours: claim it by creating an account, or tell me to delete it and it goes. Nobody who has been asked has wanted theirs removed, but the offer is real either way.

And the invitation is not conditional on the account. The listing is the thing survivors actually want — a findable list of people and what they can do. It is already doing its job.

2026-08-28 · Request For Skills: teaching and childcare

This is a Request For Skills, and it is the first one. The format is simple: name what this economy does not have, and ask people to go find it.

The first request is teaching and childcare.

Why these two

The last post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/what-about-school) worked through schooling for a survivor community and reached a plain conclusion about childcare: it sits close to babysitting, the app already handles that shape end to end, and there is nothing to build. The gap is people.

Teaching is the same. A community arrives with children in it, and neither the teaching nor the care of them is covered by anybody currently on the map.

What to pick

The list now carries both, under Education. Two occupations were added and one skill joined an occupation that already existed:

Childcare Workers — childcare and babysitting, infant and toddler care, after-school care, special needs care, child first aid and CPR.

Tutors — tutoring, and homeschooling and curriculum planning.

Vocational Trainers — adult education and training.

That second one under Tutors is worth stopping on. The last post asked whether homeschooling is what people are actually seeking and said I did not know. Somebody who homeschools can now be listed as somebody who homeschools, which is a better way to find out than asking.

How to answer it

SkillsHunt (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-hunt). You nominate somebody: a name, where they are, and what they can do. You do not have to be certain the person is a survivor — the screen says so outright. An accepted nomination grants the scout credits, once per nomination and never twice.

The mechanics are all in an earlier post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/finding-people-earns-credits).

Nominate people who teach, tutor, or look after children. Also nominate anyone else. A request naming two things is not a restriction to two things — the catalog runs to roughly 650 job titles and skills, and only about a quarter of them have anybody in them at all.

The part that makes it worth doing

The credits an accepted nomination grants you are the same credits members exchange with each other for housing, rides, requests and services. So a parent who finds and nominates a teacher earns the thing they would then exchange with a teacher.

That loop is the whole design, and it is worth being exact about where it currently stands: it does not close yet. The categories exist now, and nobody is in them. That is not a hole in the argument — it is the argument. The credits are worth what the map has on it, and the map is what a request like this is asking you to fill.

For a targeted family, closing that loop is a serious thing. Teaching and care are two of the largest recurring costs a household carries, and they are among the first things to become impossible when somebody's income is being taken from them on purpose.

Public assistance, and why there is none

Where a Targeted Individual can get help is the question this whole app exists to answer, so it is not the narrow one worth asking here. The narrow one is public assistance, and it comes up constantly.

Public assistance means something particular. It is necessary goods and services — somewhere to live, food, care for a child, medicine — supplied free or at a reduced cost to people who cannot otherwise obtain them. Not advice, not a referral, not a number to call. The thing itself, for nothing or for less.

For somebody trafficked and harassed by the Specterati, there is none of it. No agency has a category for this, no form has a box for it, and no benefit is administered against the crime as survivors describe it.

Skills Economy is not public assistance and I am not going to call it that. It is not a government program, not a benefit, not an entitlement, nobody is owed anything by it, and there are legal reasons not to claim it is equivalent to one.

What it does is the same shape of thing, reached from the opposite direction. Necessary goods and services move to the people who need them — a place to stay, a ride, a child looked after, a subject taught — and they move on credits, which are earned by doing something for somebody else rather than bought. So a member obtains what they need without money changing hands. That is comparable to what public assistance delivers. It is not administered to recipients by a program; it is supplied by survivors to each other.

It is also the only thing I know of catered to this specifically. Not a general service that hands a survivor a leaflet, but people who already know what is being done to you and do not need convincing first.

That is why a request like this matters more than it looks. Nobody is going to build this list for us.

Where to find it in the app

- SkillsHunt (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-hunt) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skills-hunt)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)
- Foundation (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/foundation) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#foundation)
- Skills Taxonomy (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-taxonomy) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skills-taxonomy)
- Commons — the group chat (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#commons)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/rfs-teaching-and-childcare

2026-08-28 · What about school?

The posts about a survivor community covered whether it is possible and who could plan it (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/a-gated-community-and-who-could-plan-it), that it has a threat model like every community does (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/every-community-has-a-threat-model), and how few people it takes (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-few-people-it-takes).

None of them mentioned school. Children come with the families, and I wrote a whole post to parents (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/your-children-can-have-an-option) about what a career could look like for a targeted child without once addressing how that child gets educated between now and then. The two-generation goal (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-two-generation-goal) is measured in exactly the span where somebody's children grow up.

So this post asks rather than answers.

The part I am not guessing about

The app is adults only. You have to be eighteen to hold an account, and that is in the terms, not a preference I could quietly relax. So a child is never a member here, never has a profile, and nothing built for education would put one in front of this app.

That is a constraint and it is also a clarifier. Anything built serves the parent who is doing the teaching — the same way the Directory serves an adult who can do a thing, not the person who benefits from it.

This app connects people; it is not where the work gets done

The second thing I am not guessing about, and it changes what is worth building.

The app puts people in front of each other. The service itself happens between them, using whatever the provider uses. Somebody running a repair business off the Directory needs inventory management, and this app has none and should not have one — they run their own, and the app finished its job when the customer found them.

So a schooling tool does not have to be part of this app, and probably should not be. The app's job is connecting a family that needs teaching to the person who can teach. What that person uses to teach is theirs — a tool I build separately, a tool that already exists, or a notebook.

That splits the question in two, and the halves have different answers.

Childcare is already answered

Worth separating out, because it is the easier one and it is nearly solved.

Childcare sits close to babysitting: one person doing something for another, for an afternoon or a day. That is the shape this app was built for, end to end. Somebody who does it is findable in the Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory), the arrangement gets made through Foundation (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/foundation), it settles in ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits), and standing comes from Trust (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust) — built out of participation that actually happened, not out of what anyone says about somebody.

So there is nothing to build for childcare. The gap is people: members who already do this and are not listed, and members willing to learn it.

That makes it a SkillsHunt (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-hunt) problem, not a software one. SkillsHunt is how somebody gets found and added, and its missions name what the economy is short of — a mission to find a doctor is already on the board for exactly that reason. Childcare is the same kind of gap and the same kind of fix. SkillUp (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skill-up) is where somebody who wants the skill rather than already having it would pick it up.

If you can watch a child for an afternoon, or you know a survivor who can, that is the ask here. It needs no new tooling and it is available now.

What I do not know

Whether homeschooling is what people are actually seeking.

I am not the person to assume from my own life. My targeting started at five and ran through school, and I would be building from that alone, which is one person's case and not a survey.

What makes it hard, from both directions

School is where a child is away from the parent all day, in a place the parent does not control, among adults the parent did not choose. For a family that is being followed, that is a real exposure, and I understand why a parent would look at homeschooling.

Homeschooling costs the one thing the targeting takes first. It needs an adult present through the day, and the operation's whole method is to remove your income and your hours until neither exists. A parent already working three unstable arrangements to keep a roof does not have a school day to give. Telling that parent to homeschool is telling them to solve the problem by having more of the resource they are being drained of.

Both of those are true at once, and I do not think the answer is the same for every family.

Registration and legal requirements also differ by country and by state, sometimes sharply. Whatever gets built cannot assume one jurisdiction.

What already exists that is close

Nothing here was built for schooling, but three things are adjacent enough to be worth naming, because they are machinery that already runs.

SkillUp (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skill-up) runs training cohorts: a curriculum, milestones that get validated, a trainer somebody claims, and credits released as milestones are met. That is structurally a course, and it is aimed at adults learning a trade.

PeerProgramming (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/peer-programming) puts people into small weekly groups with a room they can talk in. That is structurally a class of about twelve.

Skills Taxonomy (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-taxonomy) is the catalog of sectors, job titles, and skills the whole app matches against. A parent who can teach mathematics is already describable in it.

What could be built, on each side of the split

Concrete, so there is something to react to rather than an open question.

Connecting — this is the app's job, and these belong in it:

1. Matching a parent who can teach a subject with a family that needs it taught. The Directory pattern applied to teaching. The smallest useful thing on this page, and the only one that has to live here.
2. Describing teaching properly in the Skills Taxonomy, so a parent who can teach mathematics is findable as that and not buried under something else.
3. A shelf of what parents actually use — curricula, free programs, the ones that turned out to be bad. WhatWorks (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/what-works) is already one shared, survivor-verified list organized by problem, so this is a use of something that exists rather than a build.

Delivering — a separate tool, or somebody's existing one, or nothing:

4. A curriculum and records tool: what was covered, when, with the work kept. It matters if a child re-enters a school system or applies for anything later, and it is the part a family under pressure is least able to keep. This is the inventory-management case exactly — real need, wrong place for it here.
5. Shared preparation: one parent writes a unit, twenty use it. Given the hours problem above, this may matter more than anything else on the list.
6. A plain guide to the registration requirements where you are, written by people who have done it.

The split matters because it decides what I work on. Number 1 is a change to this app. Number 4 is a different piece of software with its own life, and building it inside here would make it worse at its job and this app worse at connecting.

The answer might be none of them

Some parents will not want an app anywhere near their children, and after what surveillance has done to them that is a reasoned position rather than an objection to talk somebody out of. "Build nothing, this is not for software" is a real answer and I would rather hear it now than after building.

It is also possible the need is not schooling at all, but respite — an hour, or one other adult who can be trusted with a child for an afternoon. That is the childcare answer above, and it is already available. I would not know which of the two people actually want from where I sit, which is the point of asking.

Where to send input

The group chat, Commons (https://app.chargingthefuture.com). What you use now, what you tried that failed, which of the six is worth building, or that none of them are — and which side of the split your answer sits on, because a connecting problem and a delivering problem get solved in different places.

If you are already homeschooling, you know more about this than I do. Say what you actually do — that answer counts for more here than anything I worked out from the outside.

The same question applies past schooling. If you are providing anything in this economy and the tool you need to deliver it does not exist, say so. Inventory management was the example above because it is a real gap for anyone selling goods here, and it is not the only one.

Where to find it in the app

- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- Foundation (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/foundation) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#foundation)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)
- Trust (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#trust)
- SkillsHunt (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-hunt) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skills-hunt)
- SkillUp (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skill-up) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skill-up)

2026-08-28 · You cannot argue a smear down

A question from the queue of Quora drafts I opened and never wrote. Mine sat empty from August 6, 2025:

How do I overcome a gangstalker's false narrative? How can I distract myself from their tactics?

It was asked by J H B (https://www.quora.com/profile/J-H-B-7), and the question is at https://www.quora.com/How-do-I-overcome-a-gangstalkers-false-narrative-How-can-I-distract-myself-from-their-tactics — go and read both where they stand. Quora's export kept the words of the question and not the name of the person who asked it; the name is here because the address still resolves to them. They are also in the Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory/profile/2c69e55c-9558-4418-8304-a87590507ee0) — a community-generated profile, built from what they say about themselves in public: a pharmaceutical assistant at a compounding pharmacy, studying medicine administration. It is labeled as community-generated and names who made it, it is theirs to claim whenever they want it, and theirs to have removed by asking.

[Screenshot — The question on Quora, captured August 28, 2026. Topic tags above it read Coping Mechanisms, Harassment, Gangstalkers, and seven more. The question reads: "How do I overcome a gangstalker's false narrative? How can I distract myself from their tactics?" Below it the page shows Follow with a count of 3, Request, Answer, and More, and a row reading All related (35).]

The vocabulary I use for the people running this is in the Dictionary (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/Dictionary); the question keeps the words it was asked in.

Two halves, and they are the same question. The second one decides the first.

What a false narrative is for

A smear is not an argument. It is not offered so it can be examined — it is offered so it can be repeated.

It is built to be unfalsifiable. Vague enough that nothing you produce settles it, specific enough to embarrass, and always attached to somebody who will not say who told them. So the reply that would work against an ordinary claim does nothing. Worse, replying has costs: it says the thing again in your own voice, it puts you in the frame as the person the accusation is about, and it takes the day.

Those costs are not a side effect. They are the purchase. The rebuttal is what the narrative is being spent on.

The staged kind

Mine was often built in the street rather than said behind my back. For years Specterati operators started physical fights with me in public. One threatened to hit me with a glass bottle. Another threw a full soda can at the back of my head. Around the same time they kept asking me to call the police, which is part of the same game.

The fight is not the point of the fight. The picture is. New recruits get shown a person scuffling in the street, and that picture is the narrative — manufactured in front of witnesses who were brought there to see it, so that it arrives as something they saw with their own eyes rather than something they were told.

Not supplying that is not composure and it is not turning the other cheek. It is declining to make the footage.

What actually beats one

Nothing you say. Something anyone can check without taking your word.

A dated record you own. ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) is an incident log: date, and a location if you choose one. It takes seconds and it exists whether or not anyone ever asks you for it. The value is not that it convinces a skeptic — it is that your account of your own life stops depending on your memory under stress, and it becomes something with timestamps that were not applied afterward.

Work other people can see. A Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) profile is a public listing of what you can do. It is not a rebuttal and it never mentions the accusation. It just sits there being a specific, checkable fact about you that the narrative has no answer for.

People who know you because you did something for them. This is the strongest of the three and the only one that is not a claim at all. Someone who got a ride from you, or a repair, or a place to stay, is not evaluating testimony about you. They already know. Trust (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust) is the app's version of that: standing built out of participation that actually happened, rather than out of what anyone says about you.

And one structural thing, which is why this app is built the way it is: there is no score here. No credit score, no social score, no rating, nothing about who has complained about you feeding into what you are allowed to do. Slander is exactly what scoring systems eat — you get denied and nobody tells you which file said what. An economy that scored you would import the smear and call it risk management. This one does not score (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/spend-before-you-earn), and that is a decision, not an oversight.

Everything above is checkable without trusting me either. How to check me (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-to-check-me) is the whole method.

The second half

Now the harder one: how to distract yourself from the tactics.

The word is the problem, and I do not say that to correct whoever asked it — I asked it the same way. Distraction concedes the center. It grants that the operation holds the day and asks what to do with what is left over. Something that has to be distracted from is still the subject.

Two things are true at once. You cannot decide to stop noticing — noticing is a survival function, it is often accurate, and switching it off is neither possible nor safe. But noticing does not have to be carried. That is what the log is for: put it down somewhere dated, in fifteen seconds, and stop holding it all day.

Then the rest of the day goes on something that compounds. Not a hobby to occupy you — something that is still there next month regardless of what the Specterati operators did this week.

I spent nine months getting this wrong. Almost everything I wrote in my first nine months studied the people running the operation: what their texts install, how they are recruited, whether they enjoy it, who is above them. I consider myself an independent thinker and I fell for it anyway, because it is not an intelligence test. An operation this theatrical is built to be studied. It rewards attention with fresh puzzles, and every hour spent mapping one is an hour not spent on you. The turn is dated on the public record (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/from-them-to-me), and the nine months are there so the next person can skip them.

The same shape shows up in the question of who put you on the list. It is built so you never finish it, and you do not have to settle it (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/who-put-you-on-the-list) before your life can start moving.

What none of this promises

The narrative does not disappear. People who already believe it mostly go on believing it, and some of them were never going to be reachable by evidence — believing it is what they are there for.

What changes is smaller and more useful. It stops being the only checkable thing about you. It stops setting your schedule. And the people who matter — the ones who will actually turn up with a ride or a room — are not deciding based on it, because they are looking at what you did.

The targeting is real. It is not the most interesting fact about you.

Where to find it in the app

- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- Trust (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#trust)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/you-cannot-argue-a-smear-down

2026-08-27 · Finding people earns credits

The Directory is the thing survivors ask for most: a findable list of people and what they can do. It did not appear on its own. It was built by hand — reading what people said about themselves in public, one at a time, and recording it.

SkillsHunt is that work, opened up. Anyone can do it, and an accepted nomination grants the scout credits for it.

What a nomination is

[Screenshot — The SkillsHunt Scout screen, nominating for the Summer Skills Hunt round, active 6/20/2026 to 9/1/2026. Heading: Nominate a Survivor. "Think of someone you believe may be a survivor — you don't need to be 100% certain. Their Quora profile helps verify their identity, and their skills join our economy." Fields: Full Name, required, 2 to 100 characters. Bio, optional, up to 280 characters — for example, lives in Houston, works in construction, connected through mutual contact. Quora Profile URL, social proof, highly recommended — "Quora activity helps verify this is a real person — reduces risk of trafficker infiltration." Country required; State or Region and City optional. Skills, required, picked from the taxonomy, maximum 10, with an option to select a profession that fills the skills in for you, a keyword search, and categories including Creative and Media, Education, Emergency and Reserve Roles, Energy and Utilities, Environmental and Waste Management, Finance and Public Administration, Food and Agriculture, Health, Housing and Construction, Manufacturing and Industry, Microfinance and SME Support, Mining and Extractive, Professional and Business Services, Public Safety and Justice, R&D and High-Tech, Retail and Services, Telecommunications and IT, Tourism and Hospitality, Transport and Logistics, and Water and Sanitation. Free-text skills can be added if the taxonomy has none that fit. Submit Nomination — earn points on acceptance.]

A name, where they are, and what they can do. Nothing else is asked for.

Two things on that screen are worth reading twice. You do not have to be certain the person is a survivor — the screen says so outright. And the Quora profile is asked for because activity on a real account reduces the risk of someone being planted here by the people doing the harm. It reduces the risk. It does not remove it, and nothing does; writing can be copied and an account can be built.

Skills come from a taxonomy — a fixed list of the jobs and titles a working economy needs — because a skill picked from that list can be matched against what another member is looking for. If nothing on the list fits, you can add free text, and those go into a queue for review rather than straight into the map.

Points, and what ranks them

[Screenshot — The SkillsHunt Scout Leaderboard. "Ranked by accepted points · tie-break: first-match count, then earliest submission. Pending points convert to accepted points after admin review." One entry, in first place: farah (You), 36 accepted, 36 first-matches, 612 points, plus 450 points pending review.]

Points arrive as pending and become real when a nomination is reviewed and accepted. Taxonomy skills are worth more than proposed free-text ones, because a matched skill does more work in the economy than an unmatched word does. Ties break on first-match count, then on who submitted earliest.

The round on screen is the Summer Skills Hunt, open until September 1, 2026. My own standing is what you see: 36 accepted, 612 points, 450 more pending.

Where the credits come in

Points rank you. Credits are a separate thing, and they are what this post is mostly about.

When a nomination is accepted, ServiceCredits are granted to the scout who found the person. The amount per acceptance is set for each round, and a round can also carry a ceiling on how much any one scout earns in it. The grant happens once per nomination and never twice — a nomination reviewed a second time does not grant again.

Those credits work like every other credit in the app: usable across housing, rides, skills, and requests, and sendable to another member. If your balance is empty, community credit lets you send down to −500 and repay as you earn (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/spend-before-you-earn) — scouting is one of the ways that number climbs back.

Missions

[Screenshot — The SkillsHunt Missions screen. Heading: Active Missions — "Complete missions to earn bonus points and unlock badges." One mission listed: Find a doctor, 0 of 1 complete, plus 1 point, with a Scout Now button.]

Missions point at what the economy is short of. Find a doctor is on the board because a community needs one and we do not have enough. When a specific gap opens, a mission is how it gets named instead of hoped about.

The part people ask about

Nominating someone means creating a profile for a person who has not signed up.

That profile is labeled in the app as community-generated and it names the member who made it. The person it describes can claim it at any time, and can have it deleted by asking. Nobody has asked. When the possibility of deletion has been raised, some people have been outraged at the suggestion — the profile is how other survivors find them, and taking it down takes that away.

Being listed is not a lesser version of having an account. It is the thing itself: a findable person with stated skills, which is what most survivors say they want first.

Why the work is credited

An economy needs to know who is in it and what they can do. Every accepted nomination adds one real person with real skills to a list other survivors can search — and the work of finding that person, reading what they said, and recording it accurately is work. It was being done by hand by one person. It is something the community can do for itself, and be credited for.

Where to find it in the app

- SkillsHunt (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-hunt) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skills-hunt)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)
- Skills Taxonomy (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/skills-taxonomy) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#skills-taxonomy)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/finding-people-earns-credits

2026-08-27 · Spend before you earn

Community credit is switched on. Every member can now send down to −500 ServiceCredits and repay as they earn.

Your wallet says so directly:

[Screenshot — The ServiceCredits wallet screen. Balance 136 credits, usable across all apps. Available Balance 136, In Escrow 0, Total Balance 136. Below the balance, a line reads: "Community credit: you can send down to −500 credits, then repay as you earn."]

What it is for

Until now, sending required having. If your balance was empty, you could receive help and you could offer free or barter — but you could not send credits for a ride, a repair, or a night indoors until you had earned some first.

That order is backwards for anyone who has just been made destitute. The need comes first and the earning comes later, and the gap between them is exactly where survivors get stuck. Community credit closes that gap: you can act now and settle up as work comes in.

The same line for everyone

Every member gets the same −500. Not a tier, not a reward, not something unlocked by good behavior.

There is no credit score here. There is no social score, no rating, no history of what you have posted or who has complained about you feeding into what you are allowed to do. That is deliberate and it is written into the code itself, which says in plain words that no behavioral score gates spending on this platform.

Survivors know why that matters. Being targeted means being slandered, and slander is what scoring systems eat. You are denied the apartment, the loan, the job, and nobody will tell you which file said what. An economy that scored you would import the same problem and call it risk management.

So this one does not score. Misuse is handled by the size of the line, by freezing a wallet when something is genuinely wrong, and by disputes — after the fact, on the specific case, not by ranking people in advance.

It does not create credits out of nothing

Worth being precise, because "credit" carries a lot of baggage.

When you send on community credit, your balance goes negative and the person you sent to goes positive by the same amount. The two net to zero. Nothing is issued, the total in circulation does not grow, and there is no interest — the amount you owe back is the amount you sent, and it stays that number.

What the negative balance records is simply this: the community is holding work you have not done yet. You do it, credits come in, and the number climbs back.

Where it works

Credits move across the apps, not just in one of them:

[Screenshot — The ServiceCredits Send screen. Fields for Recipient — survivor username or ID — Send with ServiceCredits, and Amount. Below, a section headed Accepted Everywhere lists LightHouse, TrustTransport, Directory, Foundation, and SocketRelay. At the bottom: "Formance Ledger — every transaction is recorded on the Formance open-source ledger. Transparent, immutable, and verifiable."]

Housing, rides, skills, requests. The same credits, whichever part of the app you are in — and every movement written to an open-source ledger rather than a private one.

How you repay

By doing the thing the economy is for:

[Screenshot — The Earn ServiceCredits screen. "A few rewards come from the platform. The rest you earn from other members." From the platform: Verify your account, +100, one-time — add your Quora profile so we can confirm you are a real person. Help another member, per exchange, ongoing — provide a service or good such as housing, transport, skills, or requests, and the member sends you credits. Take part in SkillsHunt, per acceptance, ongoing — nominate a survivor, and credits are granted when the nomination is accepted. Contribute during a fundraiser, varies, seasonal. Then: "Everything else is peer-to-peer. You earn the same way you spend — by trading with other members." Four tiles: Housing — LightHouse; Transport — TrustTransport; Services — Directory and Foundation; Requests — SocketRelay.]

A few rewards come from the platform. The rest is other members sending you credits for something you did for them. Repaying a negative balance is not a separate obligation with its own schedule — it is the ordinary business of being useful to someone.

Correction, 2026-08-28: the screen above originally showed the SkillsHunt row reading "per round", and the description here repeated it. That was never accurate — SkillsHunt has always granted credits on each accepted nomination, not once at the end of a round. The app copy has been fixed and the screenshot replaced with one taken after the fix.

The starting figures

Every number on this blog is checkable, so here is the economy on the day community credit opened:

[Screenshot — The ServiceCredits Economy screen. "ServiceCredits are usable across the plugins. They are not money and cannot be cashed out." In circulation 4,136. Total issued 4,437. Total burned 301. Held in treasury, no figure shown. On community credit 0. Moving, 30-day velocity, 0.00. Sent in last 30 days 0.]

On community credit: 0. The line opened on August 27, 2026, so that is where it starts. The screen is live in the app, and it will read differently the next time you look.

Using credits is optional

None of this is compulsory. ServiceCredits are one way to move value between survivors here — not the only one, and not a requirement.

Free and barter did not go anywhere. You can use every part of the app and never touch a credit, and nobody is ranked or treated differently for it. Community credit is the same: an option, there if you want it. Nothing obliges anyone to send on it or to carry a balance below zero. It exists so that having nothing does not stop you from acting — not to move anyone into the negative who would rather not be.

The other half of this

Community credit is the second thing that made sending possible for someone with nothing. The first was the starting balance — SocketRelay now puts something in everyone's hand (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/socketrelay-v3-credits), which covers why a needs board running on free and barter alone left out the people who had been stripped of everything to barter with.

Together they mean the same thing from two directions: you do not have to arrive with something in order to take part.

Where to find it in the app

- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)
- SocketRelay (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/socket-relay) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#socket-relay)
- LightHouse (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/lighthouse) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#lighthouse)
- TrustTransport (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust-transport) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#trust-transport)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- Foundation (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/foundation) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#foundation)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/spend-before-you-earn

2026-08-27 · Your children can have an option

On August 26, 2026 I wrote about the two-generation goal (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-two-generation-goal) — what the Du Bois cohort did in one generation, and what this community could do in two. This one is for the parents. Specifically for Targeted Individuals (TIs) raising children who are targeted too.

The part nobody has taken yet

The Skills Economy is barely tapped, and that is the opportunity.

A working economy this size needs about 650 different jobs and titles. Roughly a quarter of them have anyone in them at all. The rest of that list is empty — not competitive, empty. I go through how that list was built and where the numbers come from (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works) in its own post.

Now put a child in it. The population I model is five million people. One percent of that is 50,000 people served. That is not a side business anywhere on earth — it is a top-tier career, and the output rate the app calibrates against is Singapore's, one of the highest per worker in the world. Estonia is the other half of the comparison: 1.3 million people who came out of occupation with nothing but their own skills and built a functioning modern state, in about two generations.

A child growing up now, inside an economy their parents built, choosing from 650 roles with most of them unclaimed, serving people who need them and will not harm them. That is available. It is not a fantasy about a rescue that arrives from outside; it is arithmetic about a list that is mostly blank.

The alternative, in their own words

The other option is the life I can describe from the inside, because it is mine. Circled around the clock by organized crime. And by people like these.

I am naming one person here. Rebecca L Norrington was an acquaintance from an online social networking app — not a friend, never met in person. In the interactions we had, she lost her temper on several occasions when asked general questions about her field, being a "happiness coach." About a year after my overt targeting began around the clock, I sent her a boilerplate email asking her to delete my account from her website.

Her reply said that I threaten people, and so I deserve to be trafficked. That she had been approached by organized crime to gossip about me, that she welcomed it, and that she enjoys it. She wrote that she is a former Department of Justice employee and can leverage the agency against me at will.

She is the only person I name in this post, and the reason is simple: she put it in writing and sent it to me. Everyone else described here said what they said out loud.

One of them is blood related to me, though we have never had a relationship. My only memory of this person is watching them steal, on drugs, arguing in the same breath, and being a hot head. They insinuated that I deserve to be trafficked, on the grounds that it would never happen to them. They are a seven-time felon who received a job and friends in exchange for trafficking. Their advice to me was to avoid speaking to any other TI, because who knows how dangerous those people are.

This past Monday I went to a convenience store, arriving around 7:55 in the morning. It opens at 8. The door handles on the main entrance were gone. Confused, I started to ask whether the store would be open or closed until the repairs were done. Before I could finish the sentence he was yelling, barely controlling his body, squared up as if preparing for a physical fight, accusing me of trying to get into the store before it opened.

There are many more conversations like these.

Two things worth knowing

Another TI once asked me — on one of my deleted Quora accounts — whether they should pick up the calls and texts from high school acquaintances who kept phoning, wanting to talk and hang out. Their only memory of these people was that they were agitators, petty thieves, and drug users. I answered: do not pick up.

Two things, and the second one is why.

They accuse you of what they are. Every time.

And the people surfacing from your past — the estranged relative, the acquaintance you barely remember — are doing the same job as the stranger who follows you down the street. It is a setup. They are the criminals here, they are the ones slandering you, and they are assembling something they can point to later and say you deserved it. Which we all know is not a thing that exists. Nobody deserves that.

Who is saying it

So when I read comments from TIs on Quora and see how distraught people are, I think about who they are hearing it from.

Only criminals believe crime should happen to people. That belief is not a judgment about you; it is the permission structure they need in order to do it at all. It is how they get through the day.

Which raises the question I would ask any survivor reading this: why seek their acceptance? Operatives are recruited out of blood relatives, so I understand the pull to take it inward when it comes from family. But everyone is somebody's family. Every Specterati operator out there is somebody's sister, brother, father, or mother, and it did not stop them.

Mine started when I was five. There is no version of a five-year-old who deserves to be trafficked. Trafficking someone is a choice, made by the person doing it. As a child, I had no choice about the neighborhood I was in or the network operating in it.

The choice a child could have

That is the part that can change.

For the adults here now, the point is a more tolerable life — that is what the economy is for, today, not in some later generation. For those of you raising young children who are being targeted: your children can have an option that neither of us had. A list of work that is theirs to take. People to do it with who are not going to hurt them.

I could not opt out. In two generations, a child could.

- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-26 · From them to me

This is the third post in a row reading the Record (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/record). The first showed the span (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-record-reads-forwards) — two years, entry by entry. The second showed what the span bought (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/two-years-to-find-three-people) — three people who changed my life. This one reads it for direction, because the entries divide into a before and an after at a single point — the day the writing turned from them to me — and that dividing point is the most useful thing on the page.

The first nine months

The first entry is dated October 12, 2024. For about nine months after it, almost everything I wrote studied the people running the operation. What their texts install. How they are recruited and what they are told. Whether they enjoy it. What they want from me. Who is above them. Why they pick who they pick.

I consider myself an independent thinker, and I fell for it anyway — because it is not an intelligence test. It is the design. An operation this theatrical is built to be studied. It rewards attention with new puzzles, and every hour spent mapping them is an hour not spent on me. The operation runs on stolen attention, and for nine months it had mine.

The Record shows how long the design can hold a person when nothing interrupts it. The questions I answered came from people six years in, thirteen years in, one targeted since 2002 — most of them deep in the same questions I spent my first nine months on. That is not a verdict on any of them. It is a measure of the trap.

The turn, dated

On July 13, 2025, I posted a survey and called it "my new approach at finding out who and where we are." The lens moved off of them and onto us — who we are, where we are, what we live with.

Three weeks later, on August 3, 2025, I wrote the sentence everything since has come from:

Do you want to join a network to list what each TI is skilled in? I am being literally starved. I would like to create a TI circular economy. I was an engineer before this. There's likely a TI a mechanic, we can provide for each other.

I wrote that while being starved, and the sentence does not stay there. It moves, in the same breath, to what each of us can do. That is the turn, in one line, on one day, on the public record.

What followed is a straight line: the surveys and the map that summer, the network the same day as that sentence, the first version of the app on September 14, 2025 — six weeks from that sentence to running software — the Directory that October — a running list of Targeted Individuals (TIs) alongside their talents — the manifesto in January, the full economy of version 3 in June, this blog in August. Everything that exists today was built after the turn.

No car, no home, no job

One thing that line of dates hides. A reader could assume it was built from a stable base — a car, a home, a job. I had none of the three.

The targeting began when I was five and has cycled between covert and overt my whole life. On January 1, 2023, it restarted as 24/7 overt, and it made me destitute very fast. I spent these two years living outside. My car was stolen. Every employment opportunity was blocked. I was punched in the face, back-handed, pushed to the ground and, the same day, exposed nude. Before this cycle, I had been punched in the face and run over with a car. The Forced Homecoming scheme was run all the way to fruition, and one TI — one of the three the last post is about — helped me escape it.

Correction, 2026-09-18: this originally gave the date as January 1, 2024. The year was wrong; the overt phase restarted on January 1, 2023.

I list this for accuracy, not for rank. I have met TIs who endure the torture differently, and this is not me saying I have it worse. It is here so the two years are read as they happened: the survey, the network, the Directory, the app, and the Skills Economy were built outside, while this was going on.

The luck was manufactured

People say you cannot meet a real TI, in person or at all. The Record says otherwise, in both directions. Three real ones changed my life. I have also met an operative posing as one of us in person — the Record holds that meeting too.

The difference is what you connect to do. Connecting to talk is the easier trap: talk is open-ended, and an operative can hold a conversation for as long as it takes. Connecting to organize is harder to fake, and it is how the three found me — I was findable, daily, in public, with concrete asks and concrete offers. The skills map and the Directory exist to make that findability the default instead of a two-year project. What looks like luck was surface area.

The app retraced the same arc, version by version. Version 1 launched with a single feature, SupportMatch — peer-to-peer counseling, connection to talk. Version 2 introduced the Directory — being findable. Version 3 is a full economy — connection to organize. Each version moved the same direction the whole two years moved.

Coordination runs in parallel

I also used to think coordination was the only solution. I no longer think it is the only one. It runs in parallel with seeking justice — the report, the paper trail, the case. Neither waits on the other, and neither substitutes for the other. Building does not pause while a report is made, and making one does not pause the building.

The nine months are optional

Two years of this reads as advanced. It was one turn, held daily, from outside. Nothing in it required anything another survivor does not have — no credentials, no money, no permission, no address. And the next person does not have to buy the turn at the price I paid: the nine months are on the public record to be skipped, and the tools the turn produced are running now. You do not have to start where I started. You can start where this ends.

2026-08-26 · Seven more psyop-free games

Farah's Arcade grew on August 26, 2026: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/arcade/

The arcade calls itself "a small, honest home for free, open-source, offline-first games — with no ads, no tracking, and no harassment," and every word of that is checkable: each game carries a Play link and a Source link side by side, and the code is public. No ads, ever — nothing to buy, no nag screens. No accounts, no analytics, no cookies. Offline-first means a game loads once and then plays with no signal — on a phone with no data plan, on a bus, wherever you are.

The seven added on August 26

- 2048 — slide the numbered tiles together; two that match combine into one worth double.
- Snake — steer a snake that grows every time it eats. Do not hit the wall, and do not run into yourself.
- Breakout — slide the paddle, bounce the ball back up, and clear every brick on the screen.
- Space Invaders — rows of aliens march down toward you; shoot them all before they land.
- Pong — paddle against the computer. First to seven points wins.
- Gem Match — swap two gems to line up three or more of the same color, then watch the rest fall into place.
- Word Wheel — spin the wheel, guess a letter, and solve the hidden phrase before your turns run out.

Already on the shelf

Glowline and Glowline 2, Chess, Cascade (a falling-blocks puzzle), Redline (a fast-running platformer), and Vox — a side-scrolling platformer where you play a survivor who got their voice back and fights back, one cartoon tactic at a time. Specterrealm, a spooky realm adventure, is in progress and marked coming soon.

That makes fourteen, all playable in the browser.

Why an arcade at all

Games from the mainstream stores arrive with attachments: ads, accounts, analytics, forced updates that can change an app under you. These have none of that by construction. A page of static files cannot ask who you are, cannot phone home, and cannot change what you already loaded — what you loaded is what you play, and the source is one click away if you want to see for yourself.

The mission line has always promised psyop-free products and services. The games are the version of that you can hold today, free, without signing up for anything.

Requests

I take requests for games. If there is one you want — a classic, or an idea of your own — leave the suggestion in Commons, the community group chat.

Where to find it in the app

- Commons (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#commons)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-26 · The two-generation goal

The last post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/from-them-to-me) ended at a turn: nine months studying the people running the operation, then a shift to building for us, and the next person can start where the turn ended. This post is about where the building is pointed — the goal I have already set, and the stretch goal past it.

The goal that is set: 384

The 384 goal carries two meanings, and both are set.

The first is a product benchmark. I estimate five million survivors globally, and the app has to be built to hold that. 384 is the benchmark on the way: if the app carries 384 people trading at once, the odds of the infrastructure and features breaking at a million are that much lower, and the product has proven it is more than a niche — it is solving a real-world problem. For this meaning, 384 real versus 384 mixed real and fake is not a deal breaker: an operative on the app is momentarily adding value, and the real members and I get something out of even that.

The second meaning is personal, and stricter. The app does not verify anyone, so no screen can award this one. My personal goal is 384 people that I personally know are real, trading with each other at any given time — because I want to exit the psyop, and only real people belong in the economy I exit into. Fake ones mess up an economy.

The manifesto (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/The-Answer:-EXIT-THEIR-ECONOMY,-EXIT-THE-PSYOP) set the number in January: my goal is to connect with the 384 who are ready. The day 384 real people are trading at once, this is a working economy by anyone's definition. That is the milestone.

The stretch goal: two generations

Now the goal past that one.

W. E. B. Du Bois and his cohort — the generation that came up directly out of the transatlantic slave trade, an organized trafficking of millions — laid an economic foundation in one generation: their own schools, their own businesses, their own banks, their own press. Results in place of argument. The manifesto already stands on their method, in their own words, and it says there what stays true here: we do not compare our pain to theirs — we take the method.

Estonia is the other proof. 1.3 million people, occupied by a power that outnumbered them by more than a hundred to one and surveilled them through an informant network that reached into every town, with mass deportations to enforce it. They kept their language, kept their identity, took their country back, and continue today — a functioning state whose playbook I already study for this project's own baseline (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/TSE-Baseline).

The two extremes, at once

Those two histories are not decoration. They bracket the extremes the modern-day Targeted Individual (TI) lives in.

Being outright trafficked is the first. It happens in this community — the Forced Homecoming scheme is exactly that, and the last post records it running to fruition in my own life. Facing outsized technology built to surveil and harass, while outnumbered by the harassers by headcount, is the second. That is the ordinary condition of every TI reading this.

One group faced the first extreme and built in a generation. One group faced the second extreme and outlasted a superpower. We face both at once. So the stretch goal is two generations — the ordinary kind, twenty-five to thirty years each: what took them one, we accomplish in two. Our own economy, our own institutions, our own public record, built by people who train with their community.

And the finish line has a face: the third-generation TI. Their targeting starts, and from that moment they can exit — skipping the destitution, the sexual assault, the rape, the financial drainage that come from the Specterati. Being a TI, for them, is close to not being one. No claim that those harms cannot happen outside the operation — they can, to anyone.

If those two groups can do it, so can we.

Now, not later

A goal measured in generations can be misread as a sentence: do the work now, for strangers later, and suffer until death. Nothing would be worse to preach, and it is not what this is. Skills Economy helps now. It exists to make life more tolerable today, for the people trading in it today — that is what the trading is. The two-generation goal is the same work continuing, not the reward finally arriving.

There is a message that circulates among us: give up, the world is headed to destruction, keep your head down. The counter to it is not an argument. It is a working economy you can use today.

Where it starts

384 is the milestone inside the first generation. The trading that counts toward it is visible on Skills Economy — Live, and the people doing it are findable in the Directory. The first generation is not a plan on a shelf; it is already underway, dated, in public.

Where to find it in the app

- Skills Economy — Live (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/gdp) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#gdp)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-26 · Two years to find three people

I just published the page that shows the span (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-record-reads-forwards): two years of writing on Quora, entry by entry, dated and in order.

This post is about what those two years actually bought.

Three people

In two years of daily activity in this community, three Targeted Individuals made a life-changing impact in helping me as a targeted person.

One helped me get a job. The same person helped me not freeze to death. Another helped me escape the Forced Homecoming scheme. Another gave moral support and helped find other Targeted Individuals to start building a gated community.

Direct help, between people — none of it ran through an app, and no figure anywhere captures it. All three are listed in the Directory, and that is not a coincidence: a findable list of people and what they can do is how people like them get found at all.

Three people, found the slow way, changed what my life is like.

What the two years were

The two years were not writing. The writing is just the part that left a record. The two years were filtering, by hand, with no tools:

- Specterati operators posing as survivors — the most expensive kind of contact, because each one costs weeks before the pattern shows.
- Survivors who did not want to help. That is common, and after what people here have been through, it is theirs to decide.
- Survivors who wanted to help but could not — wrong skills, wrong place, nothing to spare.
- And survivors who wanted to and could. Three of them.

Every survivor who goes looking for help runs this same filter, alone, from scratch. Two years is what it cost me. Some people run it longer and find nobody, not because nobody exists but because the search is that bad.

What Skills Economy is

Skills Economy is the culmination of that work and that generosity — the thing the three of them and those two years added up to.

It replaces the filter. Membership is invite-only. The Directory lists who is here and what they can do, which handles the third category — you look up the skill instead of discovering by trial who holds it. And the people on the list are there because they chose to be findable, which is the fourth category self-selecting in.

Foundation is where the people who can offer help stand ready to give it: providers list what they offer, and you reach one directly and start a conversation or a call. The person on the other end is there because offering help is the thing they signed up to do.

The goal it is built around: you land, and within twenty-four hours you have assistance or guidance from people who understand what is happening to you — and no Specterati operators anywhere in the loop. That is the design target, stated as one. Two years, compressed to a day, because one person already spent the two years so the next one does not have to.

Where to find it in the app

- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)
- Foundation (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/foundation) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#foundation)
- Commons — the group chat (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#commons)
