2026-08-25 · A gated community, and who could plan it

A gated community for Targeted Individuals is one of the most common things people ask for on Quora.

This July, KC asked it outright in a survivor space (https://uspsyopstargetedindividuals.quora.com/Are-there-actual-physical-communities-of-Targeted-Individuals-living-together-protecting-one-another-2): "Are there actual physical communities of Targeted Individuals living together protecting one another?" Their answer under it was one line — "If this does exist, I want to know!"

[Screenshot — KC's question in the US-PSYOPS-TARGETED-INDIVIDUALS space on Quora: "Are there actual physical communities of Targeted Individuals living together protecting one another?" Answered by KC, Jul 8: "If this does exist, I want to know!" 309 views, 7 upvotes, 23 comments, 2 shares, 1 of 4 answers.]

People lined up in the comments. One wrote that Targeted Individuals are the only people they want to be around, that they could not trust anyone who has not been targeted, and that such a community would grow as more people are subjected to this. Others volunteered by name: willing to meet in a neutral place, a safe house in every community, land within reach of family, "I can surely benefit from a TI Community."

[Screenshot — The full comment thread under the question: another answer reading "We could all form together and fund a safe haven. Yes, people talk a lot about it and wish for it, but we have to form together and put it into action"; a commenter recommending Farah Brunache as someone "who has started to set up such a system for people needing support — this is what we need to do, band together and get this stopped"; and several people volunteering interest by name.]

One disclosure about that screenshot: it shows the thread as it stood, including a law-office flyer posted into it. I am showing the thread as evidence that people want this, and that is all — I do not know the people in it and I do not support or vouch for anything advertised there.

My answer to the question is that a gated community is possible. Not by me alone, and not as one place somewhere — but possible. And the app now shows who could plan one, which is the part of the answer I can demonstrate rather than assert.

The most common answer, and why it is not grounded

The most common answer survivors give each other is no — that the moment a community of targets formed, it would be found, infiltrated within a day, and destroyed. I have read that answer many times, from people who genuinely mean it and who have every reason to expect the worst.

The opinion is common. It is not grounded, and one detail shows how ungrounded it is: the destruction is always described as if it already happened. Under one version of that answer this July, someone asked the only question that matters — where did this happen? There was no answer, because it has not happened. It is a prediction dressed as a memory, and a prediction is exactly the kind of claim you weigh against the record.

The record says the opposite.

Marginalized groups live together today. Jewish communities exist. Chinatowns exist. The list goes on, and every group on it has enemies. Hate-group violence against them never ceases — and the communities continue anyway, decade after decade, because a neighborhood is not an organization. An organization can be beheaded. A neighborhood of people who know each other, trade with each other, and watch each other's streets does not fall apart because one bad actor walks in. Estonia is 1.3 million people with a hostile neighbor on its border, and it continues.

The history runs the other way too. Black Americans were sold integration, and it failed — to the point where it is now difficult to keep their neighborhoods from being ruined. Gentrification is the deliberate breaking up of communities that concentrated, and the effort spent on it tells you concentration works. The generation W.E.B. Du Bois wrote about built trade schools and businesses by training with their own community, and the manifesto (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/The-Answer:-EXIT-THEIR-ECONOMY,-EXIT-THE-PSYOP) covers what they did and how. What diluted their unity and prosperity was dispersal, not concentration.

So: yes, a physical place can be attacked, and infiltration is a real problem — a later section is about exactly that. But the people running this want survivors diluted and isolated. That is not a reason to stay scattered. It is the reason to go the opposite direction.

I am not writing this to argue with anyone who has settled on staying apart. That distance is common, and it is not mine to judge. This section is for the reader on the fence — the one who wants this and has been told, as if it were history, that it cannot exist. Nobody can point to the event that settled that, because there is no such event. What can be pointed to is every community on the list above, still standing.

What the question is actually asking for

People asking usually picture the finished thing: a gate, a road, houses, neighbors who believe them.

What comes before any of that is a document. Who does what, in what order, on what money, on what land, under what rules for who gets in. Every community that ever got built had one, whether or not anyone called it that.

That document is not one person's job. It needs someone who can form a legal entity and write membership criteria. Someone who can read a title and a zoning map. Someone who can cost out water, power, and waste. Someone who can plan a care model and a quiet room. Someone who can plan a gate.

And it needs someone to write all of it down in a form a different group can pick up and use somewhere else entirely. That last part matters most. One runbook, reusable anywhere in the world, is worth more than one compound in one state.

The Community tab

The Workforce screen in the app has a tab called Community. It sorts the people already on the skills map into the ten teams that document needs.

Nobody signs up for it. It is not a second roster and it is not a second application. Everyone in Community is already in the Directory — it is the Directory, filtered on the categories a gated community would need.

The screen says so itself: "Each team draws from the Workforce sectors it maps to; the roster is every member who already matches those sectors. This recomputes live from the Directory — it updates itself as members and skills change."

To reach it, scroll the tab strip at the top sideways. This is the Workforce screen as it opens:

[Screenshot — The Workforce screen as it opens. The tab strip reads Overview, Sectors, Skill Level, Occupations, with no Community tab visible. Four tiles: Population 5,000,000, survivor population baseline; Workforce Total 2,000,000, 40% participation; Recruited 100, 0% of goal; Skills Coverage 24%, 159 of 650 skills. Then the Skills Economy Summary card: "With 100 people recruited, we have reached 24% of the skills potential of an independent nation state like Finland, Estonia, or Singapore — equating to $14.3 million in GDP potential. That means each individual contributing $142,500 in GDP, and earning upwards of $71,250." The card carries its own caveat: these figures are speculative, this is the only place in the app where GDP is stated in US dollars, and the Skills Economy has no intention of forming a nation state. Below, a skill level breakdown, sector-by-sector recruited counts against goals, and top training opportunities.]

The strip reads Overview, Sectors, Skill Level, Occupations. Community sits off the right edge. Drag the strip left and it appears. I say that plainly because a reader who cannot find a tab concludes it does not exist.

The ten teams

[Screenshot — The Community tab: ten planning teams with member counts. Legal and Governance, 12 members — entity formation, membership and gating criteria, resident agreements, liability, dispute process. Finance, 14 — funding model, budget, land purchase financing, ongoing cost model, reserves. Land and Site, 9 — site criteria, land search, zoning, water and soil and environmental checks, utilities feasibility. Build and Infrastructure, 9 — site plan, housing design, build order, roads, power, water, waste, connectivity. Food and Agriculture, 7 — growing capacity, food storage, kitchen and commons planning. Health and Wellbeing, 19 — care model, first aid capacity, mental-health support, quiet and recovery spaces. Safety and Security, 1 — gate and perimeter model, emergency plans, drills, incident process that respects member privacy. Technology, 6 — connectivity, community use of this platform on site, records, backups. Communications and Documentation, 36 — the runbook itself: editing every team's section into one coherent, reusable document. Operations and Maintenance, 13 — move-in logistics, shared-resource scheduling, maintenance rosters, supply purchasing.]

A person can appear on more than one team — the sectors overlap, and the app counts each roster on its own.

Open a team and you get the people, by name, with the reason each one is there:

[Screenshot — The Operations and Maintenance team expanded into its roster. Each member is a card headed by their Directory name, followed by the occupations they match and how. The first three: 00, one matching occupation — Construction Laborers, Housing and Construction, skill match via Site safety practices — with all their listed skills; Alani, one matching occupation — Construction Laborers, skill match via Home Improvement — with skills in health, manufacturing, first aid and CPR; Alicia Macchione, four matching occupations across Retail and Services, each labeled skill match or sector match with the listed skill that produced it. The roster continues in the same form.]

Every placement carries its reason. "Skill match via Site safety practices" means that person listed site safety practices, in their own public words, and that is the skill the occupation needed. Nothing here is my opinion about who belongs where, and anyone can check any row of it.

The question everyone asks second

Who gets in.

It is not theoretical and I have already had to answer it. In November 2025 a person posing as a Targeted Individual came to me wanting a spot in exactly this kind of community — one organized entirely through Quora. He spent weeks in the messages first. I wrote it up at the time: Damon Mayle, posing as a TI (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/discourse-migrate/damon-mayle-posing-as-a-ti).

My answer then is my answer now, from that post:

"Vetting" TIs is what I signed up for in the TI economy. And by vetting I mean meeting in person. That is the only way I can tell if someone is a TI or not. Most Quora posts, DMs, calls, video chats (which I will never do) are mostly meaningless. Easy to fake being a TI. Anyone can copy and paste real TI posts and mannerisms and use it as their own. You cannot fake authenticity in person.

That is the work behind the Safety & Security team's line: gate and perimeter model, emergency plans, drills, and an incident process that respects member privacy. The 2025 attempt is also why I do not think a community like this can be organized on a platform. A platform is where the people trying to get inside it already are.

And notice what that attempt actually proves. One infiltrator tried, in person, and was identified in an afternoon — by exactly the vetting the doubters say is impossible. The community was not destroyed. There was nothing to destroy yet, and the method for keeping it that way worked on its first test.

Where the numbers come from

The Workforce screen reads 100 recruited and 24 percent skills coverage — 159 of the 650 things a working economy this size needs. Where 650 comes from, where 5 million comes from, and how a person ends up on the map at all: How the math works (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works). Community is the same map, cut to one question.

Where to find it in the app

- Workforce (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/workforce) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-25 · Every community has a threat model

Earlier today, August 25, 2026, I published the case for a gated community and who could plan one (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/a-gated-community-and-who-could-plan-it). This post is about the part of that conversation that deserves its own page: threats, and what serious people do about them.

Objections are not the problem

I do not think objections to a survivor community are a problem. Communities are a serious undertaking and the objections deserve serious answers.

The problem is how the objections arrive: the threat is presented, and the other side never is. History has shown that oppressed in-person communities can work despite disruption — that is the record, and I went through it in the last post. Violence against communities will always happen. Both of those things are true at once, and a person who only ever hears the first half is not being informed. They are being frightened.

Presenting the danger with no plan beside it is not caution. It is fear dressed as caution, and its effect — whoever intends it — is that nothing ever gets built.

Every community has faced this

Every community since the beginning of time has had to consider threats. Man-made and natural. Fire, flood, drought, crime, war, sickness, the neighbor who wants the land. There has never been a community that did not face them, and there has never been a community that was built by waiting for the threats to end first.

A survivor-only community faces the same question every community has ever faced. Not a new one.

And the threats we face are not special to us. The places run by the people doing this face fire, crime, betrayal, and infiltration like everyone else does. The difference between a community that lasts and one that does not has never been the absence of threats. It has been the quality of the plan.

The plan is the answer

What matters is planning and preparedness, built on the community's own threat model — a plain list of what could go wrong, how likely each thing is, and what the community does about each one. Every functioning town, congregation, and campus on earth runs on one, written down or not.

You put your best foot forward in the plan. And when something goes wrong — something will — you fix what broke and update the next plan. That is not failure. That is the entire method, the same one every fire code and every emergency drill came from. A plan is not a promise that nothing happens. It is the reason something happening does not end the community.

Refusing to build a community because something could go wrong is like refusing to step outside for fear a tree falls on you. The fear names a real thing. Trees do fall. And staying indoors forever is still not a reasonable response to trees.

The comparison nobody makes

I have never lived in a survivor community. My life is threatened every day anyway.

That is the comparison the fear skips over. The question is not "community with threats, or safety without them?" Safety without them is not on the table — not for me, and not for the people asking on Quora whether such a community exists. The question is whether living among people who understand what is happening, with a plan, is more dangerous than living scattered among the people doing it, with none. Stated plainly, the claim that it gets worse when you leave the people harming you is not logical.

The tool

This is why the planning tool exists. The Community tab sorts the people already on the skills map into the ten teams a community's planning document needs — and one of the ten is Safety & Security, whose whole job is the threat model: the gate and perimeter model, emergency plans, drills, and an incident process that respects member privacy. The full walkthrough is in the last post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/a-gated-community-and-who-could-plan-it).

Plan first, in public, with the people who can do the work named next to the work. That is the other side that never gets presented.

Where to find it in the app

- Workforce (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/workforce) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/every-community-has-a-threat-model

2026-08-25 · How few people it takes

Many survivors do not realize how close we are.

Not close to the end of the targeting. Close to being able to live: getting work, rides, housing, and repairs from people who understand the situation.

The two numbers we are up against

Two groups outnumber the people building this.

The first is the people running the operations.

The second is survivors who want nothing to do with other survivors. I have met them in person. The distance is common.

So the people trying to build something are outnumbered twice over. Against those two numbers, a hundred people sounds like nothing.

It is not nothing.

The number

A hundred people exchanging with each other at a time is enough to run an economy a person can live inside.

Not a hundred sign-ups, and not a hundred names on a list. A hundred people trading — work done, rides given, rooms rented, things repaired, food grown — at any given moment.

Not the same hundred

This is the part that changes how the number reads, so it comes second rather than last.

It does not have to be the same hundred people. No economy anywhere requires that. A town does not have all of its residents trading every day. A shop serves different customers on Tuesday than it did on Monday and is still a business on Wednesday. Work and custom rotate through a population, and that rotation is the ordinary shape of an economy rather than a weakness in one.

So a hundred is a throughput figure, not a membership roll. It asks how many are exchanging at once — not who they are, and not whether they keep it up.

Nobody has to commit to being one of a fixed hundred forever. Nobody's bad month breaks it. People come in, do what they can while they can, and step back when they need to, and the number holds because somebody else is trading that week.

That is why a hundred is reachable, and why a fixed hundred would not be.

What a hundred gets you

At that size, a person can post that they need work and have it answered. Take a ride from someone who was vetted rather than from a Specterati operator. Find a room. Find someone who can fix the thing that broke, in exchange for something they can do back.

Not a movement, not a verdict on anyone, not an ending. A person going about an ordinary week without every transaction running through people who are paid to make their life worse.

Living instead of enduring.

Why a hundred is enough

The math is short enough to do right here.

Singapore runs a pure skills economy and publishes its numbers. Take its total output and divide by its workers, and one person's skills produce about $142,500 a year.

A hundred people exchanging full time at that rate is about $14.3 million a year moving between them.

The app runs this calculation live, and on August 25, 2026 it happens to be sitting at exactly this number:

[Screenshot — Four tiles: Population 5,000,000, survivor population baseline; Workforce Total 2,000,000, 40% participation; Recruited 100, 0% of goal; Skills Coverage 24%, 159 of 650 skills. Below them, the Skills Economy Summary card: "With 100 people recruited, we have reached 24% of the skills potential of an independent nation state like Finland, Estonia, or Singapore — equating to $14.3 million in GDP potential. That means each individual contributing $142,500 in GDP, and earning upwards of $71,250." The card's own note underneath: "These figures are speculative, not actuals, and this summary is the only place in the app where GDP is stated in US dollars. The Skills Economy has no intention of forming a nation state — this is a baseline for understanding economics at the scale of upwards of 5 million people."]

Fourteen million dollars a year circulating among a hundred people is not a support group with a spreadsheet. It is an economy — enough for work to be findable, rides to be offered, rooms to be rented, and repairs to be traded, week after week, without anyone going outside it for the basics.

To be plain about what that figure is: it is what a hundred people's skills are worth at the output rate of one of the best skills economies on earth. It is a measure of capacity, not money sitting in anyone's hands. The full derivation — where the rate comes from, why Singapore, what the comparison does and does not claim — is here: How the math works (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works).

What people are waiting for

My impression is that a lot of people are holding out for world peace and an end to human trafficking inside their own lifetime.

I do not expect either. And I think expecting it is exactly what makes people give up on the smaller thing that is actually available — because measured against an ending that is not coming, a hundred people trading looks like nothing worth getting out of bed for.

Measured against a week you have to get through, a hundred people trading changes everything.

It has been done before

A Specterati operator named Damon Mayle once told me, in as many words, that I am the first person to ever attempt this. I am recounting it from my own conversation with him; the Quora account it was said on has since been erased, so there is nothing to link yet. He is the same person who posed as a Targeted Individual to get a spot in a gated community (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/discourse-migrate/damon-mayle-posing-as-a-ti).

He was half right, and the half he got wrong is the useful half.

First within this psyop — possibly. First at all — no. Race was one of the many psyops before this one, and it had its Targeted Individuals. In 1903 a persecuted community in America published a book of essays about precisely this problem: people denied work, housing, fair courts, and physical safety by an organized system most of the country refused to fight. Their answer was not to wait for rescue. It was skills, an economic foundation, leadership from within, a public record, and results in place of argument.

The manifesto goes through what they wrote, quotes them directly, and links the full free text. I am not going to restate it here: Live, Work, and Prevail (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/The-Answer:-EXIT-THEIR-ECONOMY,-EXIT-THE-PSYOP).

I will repeat one line from it, because this post is the reason it is in there: we do not compare our pain to theirs. We take the method. Nothing here ranks anyone's suffering against anyone else's. A method can be inherited without that.

The reason survivors miss the inheritance is optics. The reasoning runs: I am not Black, so nothing happening to me resembles any of that. What it costs is the method — a record of people who reached freedom in the middle of chaos and destruction and wrote down how, in detail, in public, in a book you can download for free this afternoon.

And they did not get an ending either. What they got was one generation, and trade schools, and businesses, and communities that existed where none had. Estonia took about two generations to build a modern state out of nothing but what its people knew. Neither of them waited for the thing to stop first.

What isolation is for

A person who believes they are the only one suffers more than a person who knows they are not. That is not a figure of speech.

Isolation is not a side effect of this. It is the mechanism. Keeping each of us convinced we are alone with it is most of what the work consists of, and it is the part that a hundred people trading with each other takes apart directly — not by arguing anyone out of anything, but by putting a person in a week where other survivors are simply, unremarkably present.

Not a demand

I am not asking anyone to feel differently about what happened to them, and staying apart from other survivors is a common thing to do after what a lot of people have been through.

The door is open, the number is a hundred, and nobody has to be the same hundred twice.

I think what is on the other side of this is fewer mass shootings, fewer people taking their own lives, and people thriving despite human trafficking. I cannot prove that and I am not going to pretend to. It is what I expect, and I would rather say so than keep it to myself.

Where to find it in the app

- SocketRelay (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/socket-relay) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#socket-relay)
- TrustTransport (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/trust-transport) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#trust-transport)
- LightHouse (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/lighthouse) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#lighthouse)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-25 · Looking Up This Blog in the Wayback Machine

An earlier post said the Internet Archive holds copies of what is written here, and that you should check rather than take my word for it. It gave one address as an example.

This is the rest of it. How to look up any post, how to find the address yourself, and how to read what comes back.

Nothing here needs an account or any tool beyond a browser.

The one-minute version

Go to https://web.archive.org

Paste this into the search box and press enter:

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/wiki-site/content/posts/how-to-check-me.md

You will get a calendar of dates. Each highlighted date is a day the Internet Archive took a copy. Click one and you are reading that post exactly as its text stood on that date, from a copy I did not make and cannot edit.

Everything below is how to do it for any other post, and how to know what you are looking at.

Two more you can try immediately

These are archived and were checked by hand:

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/wiki-site/content/archive/discourse/Home.md
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/wiki-site/content/archive/discourse/book-2-backyard-farming.md

And the blog's front page, which is a normal web page rather than a text file:

https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/

What you are actually looking at

The address above is not the page you read on the blog. It is the source file — the post as a plain text file, sitting in a public repository.

That is deliberate, and it is the better thing to check. The rendered page is a presentation of the text. The file is the text. If you want to know whether a sentence was there on a given date, the file answers it and the page only shows it to you.

It also means the copy is legible without any of my code running. Open a capture from six months from now, or from after this project has stopped existing, and it is still just words in a file.

Finding the address of any post yourself

Three steps, and the third is a button.

One. Open the repository: https://github.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site

Two. Navigate to the post. Posts live in wiki-site/content/posts/. Archived material from the old forum is in wiki-site/content/archive/discourse/, and archived Quora material will be in wiki-site/content/archive/quora/. The file names are readable — how-to-check-me, a-safe-place-to-shower — so you can usually find a post by its title.

Three. Open the file and click the Raw button, at the top right of the file view. The address bar now holds the raw address. Copy it, paste it into web.archive.org.

That is it. You never have to construct an address by hand.

If you would rather build the address

The pattern is:

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/ + the file's path in the repository

One thing will trip you up, and it trips me up too. The repository is called wiki-site, and inside it there is also a folder called wiki-site. So the word appears twice in every address, and an address with only one of them will not work:

.../chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/wiki-site/content/posts/how-to-check-me.md

If a lookup returns nothing, check that first. It is almost always this.

Reading the calendar

After you search an address, the Internet Archive shows a timeline of years and a calendar. Highlighted days are days it holds a capture. Hovering shows the times; clicking opens that copy.

A few things worth knowing so the result does not mislead you.

A post will usually have a capture from around the day it was published, because this blog submits pages to the Internet Archive automatically when it publishes.

Older posts may have fewer captures than recent ones. That is not a signal about the post. The automatic submission was fixed recently, and everything published before that was backfilled by hand afterward — so the dates cluster around when the backfill ran rather than when each post was written.

A missing capture proves nothing. The Internet Archive rate-limits, and refuses saves when it is busy. A page with no capture is a page whose submission did not get through, not a page that was hidden. Read absence as absence of evidence, not evidence of absence.

Comparing a capture against now

This is the part that actually checks something.

Open the capture. Open the same address live in another tab — the raw address, not the blog page. Read both.

If the text is identical, the post has not changed since that date. If it differs, you are looking at exactly what changed, and the repository's history will tell you when and in which commit.

Two kinds of page here change on purpose, and you should expect them to differ: the Dictionary and the page tracking dead and current links are kept current rather than frozen. Everything else — the arguments, the announcements, the progress reports — is fixed once published. A difference in one of those is worth asking me about.

The one thing that does not work

If you paste a normal article address into web.archive.org, like this one:

https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-to-check-me

you will find nothing. Not a gap — nothing, ever.

Here is why, plainly. This blog does not keep a separate file on the server for each post. It sends your browser a small program, and that program assembles the page in front of you from the text. When the Internet Archive's crawler asks the server for that address, the server has nothing at that address to hand over, so the capture fails every time.

The front page is different and does archive, because it is a real address the server answers.

So: front page and raw text files, yes. Article pages, no. That is not a limitation anyone chose, and the raw file is the better record anyway.

What this proves, and what it does not

It proves a specific text existed at a specific address on a specific date, and that a third party with no relationship to me holds the copy.

It does not prove the text is true. A dated copy of a claim is still a claim.

The earlier post covers that boundary properly, along with the other ways to check this work — the repository's own history, the app's public code, and the numbered feed:

https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-to-check-me

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/looking-up-this-blog-in-the-wayback-machine

2026-08-25 · The audience is already larger than the goal

My goal is 384 approved members working with each other at any given time. I have written about where that number comes from (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works); this post is about whether it is reachable.

I think it is, and I am going to show the evidence rather than assert it.

451

The Skills Economy space on Quora has been followed by 451 people, all time.

[Screenshot — The Skills Economy space's statistics screen on Quora, all-time view, with the space header reading "first-ever psyop-free economy. all posts survive here / chargingthefuture.com/feed." The tiles: Views 78,977 · Follows 451 · Upvotes 1,387 · Comments 1,439.]

[Screenshot — The rest of the same statistics screen, reached by scrolling the tile row sideways: Follows 451 · Upvotes 1,387 · Comments 1,439 · Content 938.]

The stats row scrolls sideways on Quora, so the two images together are the screen in its entirety.

That reading is from the evening of August 24, 2026. The figures move daily, so by the time you look they will read differently — the screen updates after 8:00 pm Eastern each day.

Let me be precise about what that number is.

A follow is not a member. It is not a sign-up, and it is not a person on the skills map. It is a person who saw this work and chose to keep seeing it. Evidence of reach, nothing more.

But look at what it means for the goal. 451 is more than 384. The people the goal needs are not hypothetical, not projected from a growth curve, not waiting to be discovered. An audience larger than the goal has already been reached once — by accounts that kept getting erased, which is the subject of the next section.

Reached under fire

Every follower that space has was gained while the personal accounts posting into it kept being erased.

Quora has banned eight of my accounts. The full dated record is on old links, new links (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/old-links-new-links), which is kept current so you can check the sequence yourself rather than take my word for it.

The pattern I observe is that the erasures track milestones. The clearest case is documented to the minute: on September 14, 2025, my first account posted the app's first release announcement, and seventeen minutes later the account was banned and everything it had ever posted was deleted. The launch date is independently on the public record — a Discourse post from five days later, archived on this blog (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/discourse-migrate/support-match-waitlist), says the app "was released on September 14th, 2025."

I state plainly that I cannot prove the connection. A ban never arrives with its real reason attached, and that unprovability is part of how this works. What I can do is date everything and keep the record public, which is done.

Through all of it, the space stood. The accounts die; the 451 people who chose to follow the work are still there.

The other number is my own work

The skills map currently holds 100 people, and that number is a different kind of claim.

Every person on it is someone I researched individually — skills stated in their own words, in public — and placed on the map with a community-generated Directory profile, labeled as such, claimable by them, removable on request. I do this daily.

So when I say 384 is reachable, I am not claiming I can persuade 384 strangers of anything. I am claiming I can keep doing work I have already done 100 times, aimed at an audience that already numbers 451. One claim is salesmanship. The other is arithmetic about my own hands.

Why the first account mattered most

The account banned seventeen minutes after the launch had something none of the others did: reach outside the survivor community. Its readers did not arrive expecting a conspiracy account, and it was not read as one.

That is exactly the thing being prevented. An account that reads as a conspiracy account is dismissed without argument — no ban needed. An account that does not is a problem.

It is also why this project has a Dictionary (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/Dictionary). I abandoned the inherited vocabulary and built my own terms, because the inherited words mark the writer as dismissable before the first sentence is read. The Dictionary is not housekeeping. It is a countermeasure.

What this adds up to

The goal is 384. The audience already reached is 451. The map already holds 100, added by one person working daily. The record of what it cost to get here is dated and public.

None of those numbers is a promise. Together they are why I think the goal arrives.

Where to find it in the app

- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- Workforce (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/workforce) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-audience-is-already-larger-than-the-goal

2026-08-25 · The Record reads forwards

The blog has a new page: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/record

It is called The Record, and it answers a question the feed cannot. The feed runs newest-first and answers what is being said now. The Record runs oldest-first and answers how long it has been said, how much of it there was, and how many places it reached.

What is on it

The writing I scattered across Quora — not the posts to my own spaces, which are the blog's own archive material, but the writing that lived on other people's pages: answers under their questions, comments under their answers and posts, submissions to their spaces.

As I write this, the page counts 632 entries, under 149 different questions, across 48 spaces, spanning 22 months — from October 2024 to today. The page computes those numbers live from the files it reads, so by the time you look they may have grown.

That kind of writing never reads as a body of work while it is happening. Each piece sits alone under somebody else's question, and no one — including the writer — sees the whole. Put back in order, it is the whole: the better part of two years of showing up, answering, and building in public, day over day.

Why the stats matter

Anyone can say they have been at something for two years. The Record does not say it. It shows it, entry by entry, each one dated, each one carrying the address it originally lived at.

Those original addresses print as plain text, never as links — every one of them is dead. Quora destroyed the two accounts that held this writing, and the page draws both takedowns across the timeline at the date they happened. What the timeline shows next is the point: the entries continue past each one. The dated running list of every erased account stays on old links, new links (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/old-links-new-links); The Record deliberately carries no such count, because that number moves and this page is about the thing that does not — the span.

Consistency is the one credential nobody can fake backwards. A new account can be opened in an afternoon. Twenty-two months of dated writing under other people's questions cannot. When you are deciding how much weight to give what someone builds and says, how long they have kept at it — visibly, checkably — is worth more than any single claim they make. That is the stat this page exists to show, and it is why I keep every number on this blog checkable rather than asserted.

How it fits

The pipeline is the same one the whole blog runs on now. The text on The Record is the raw export from Quora's own records, restored file by file; as each entry is copy-edited it is replaced in place, and the page shows the edited wording the moment that happens. Everything is in the public repository with its history, so the restoration itself is on the record too.

Read it forwards, from the first entry. That is the way it happened.

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-record-reads-forwards

==============================================================================
2026-08-25 · Why nothing is written on a platform first

There is a standing rule on this blog, stated on old links, new links (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/old-links-new-links): nothing is written on a platform first anymore. Every post starts here, and platforms only ever get a short version and a link back.

A rule on its own reads as policy. With its history attached, it reads as what it is — a lesson that cost two archives to learn. Here is the history.

Stage one: everything on Quora, no plan

This project started on Quora with no publishing strategy at all. I wrote where the readers were, and every word lived in one place: on the platform, in that account.

I fumbled through it. There was no procedure, no second copy, no thought given to what would happen if the account went away. It did not seem like a thing that needed thinking about.

On September 14, 2025, that account posted the announcement of the app's first release. Seventeen minutes later, Quora banned it. A Quora ban deletes everything the account ever posted, so months of writing went in an evening — the announcement included.

The dated record of that account and every one after it is on old links, new links.

Stage two: a backup, pointed the wrong way

Four days later I opened a Discourse forum. This was the first attempt at owning a copy of my own writing, and it half worked.

Half, because of the order. I kept writing on Quora first and copy-pasting into Discourse after. The backup was downstream of the thing that kept getting erased — anything Quora removed between my writing it and my copying it was gone, and the copy's existence depended on my remembering to make it. That is the wrong way round, and I say so now because at the time it did not occur to me that the order mattered.

The forum closed on December 23, 2025. Its posts were exported and live in this blog's archive (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/discourse-migrate/Home) — which is more than can be said for anything that lived only on Quora.

Stage three: the same lesson, taught again

On August 16, 2026, Quora banned the account I had been writing on since — same signature, everything it ever posted deleted, the handle and its space left standing and bare.

The first erasure had already taught this lesson. I had responded with a backup, which was an answer to the wrong question. The question was never "do I have a copy?" It was "which copy is the source?"

That evening I stopped adjusting and changed the order. The blog became the source: every post starts as a file in a public repository, with a timestamped history of when it was written and how it changed. Platforms get an excerpt and a link back, written after the post exists, never before. The full arrangement is in Our writing has a permanent home (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/our-writing-has-a-permanent-home), posted the day after.

What the order changes

The difference is not where the writing sits. It is what an erasure can reach.

Under the old order, the platform held the original, so an erasure took the original. Under this order, a platform holds nothing but pointers. When the next account is erased — and I assume there will be a next time — the cost is one entry moving from the current list to the dead list on old links, new links. The writing, the screenshots, the history: untouched.

Eight accounts have now been erased, and the difference is visible in what survives. From the Quora-only era, nothing but what other people happened to quote. From the Discourse era, everything that got copied in time. From the blog-first era, everything, with its history, in a copy nobody can quietly empty.

It took two erasures to learn a rule that fits in a sentence. It is on the record now so nobody else building something under the same conditions has to pay twice for it: write on ground you hold, and hand the platforms a copy.

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/why-nothing-is-written-on-a-platform-first

2026-08-24 · The Assistant Is Not a Clinician

On August 23, 2026 I asked people to lend their writing to the Knowledge Library, and explained what it is: a private assistant for Targeted Individuals that answers from the writing of people who have lived this.

Ione (https://www.quora.com/profile/Ione-40) replied, and the reply was the right one.

[Screenshot — A comment thread on Quora. Ione writes: Not suicidal. If anyone needs help I will pass on this information, once I do my fact check. Kingmingling replies: Thank you. Right now what happens is if someone needs help they call a national hotline and told to suck it up (like what I encountered). Or other avenues that say you're delusional. This AI assistant is 24/7 and does not lie about organized harassment and instead provides answers and advice based on TI writings. If you would like your writings used to help others across the globe please submit them. Note neither myself or the bot claims to be mental health professionals. Merely an alternative to commercial bots and professionals that lie about human trafficking which leads to suicides and mass shootings.]

Not suicidal, and: I will pass this on once I do my fact check.

Check it. That is exactly the correct response to somebody telling you there is a tool that can help a person in trouble. Nothing in this post asks anyone to take it on faith, and the people who check before passing something on are the ones worth listening to.

What a person actually meets when they need help

I called a national hotline. What came back was, in substance, join the club — this happens to a lot of people, and you should change your daily routine so they cannot follow you.

Both halves of that are worth pulling apart, because together they are worse than either alone.

The first half concedes the whole thing. It is common. Whoever said it was not disputing that organized harassment happens; they were telling me I was one of many. And having conceded it, they offered nothing. That is not a training gap. That is flippancy, and a person calling a hotline can tell the difference immediately.

The second half is advice that does not work, and my own writing is the evidence for that rather than my opinion. Change your routine. I travel, and it travels with me. What varies when I move is which day gets staged, not whether it happens. A routine is not what makes somebody findable, so changing it does not make them unfindable — it just puts the work of fixing this back onto the person it is being done to.

Then there is the part that matters more than either half.

I had gone to a police station to file a report. People were walking into me on purpose, in view of cameras, trying to get me to hit back so the footage would show a violent Black woman. While I was there, an officer mocked that hotline call back to me, in person. One of them.

So the call did not stay where I made it. The number a frightened person is told to ring reached the people doing this, and came back at me as a joke, from someone with a badge.

Other avenues do the reverse and end up in the same place: describe what has been happening and be told you are delusional. Your account becomes the symptom, and everything you say after that is heard through that frame, including the parts anybody could go and check.

What the assistant is instead

It answers at any hour, because it does not keep hours.

It does not treat a description of organized harassment as evidence that the person describing it is unwell. It answers from what Targeted Individuals have written about what happened to them and what they did about it — the practical part, from people who were there.

It is a narrow claim and it is deliberately narrow.

What it is not, and what I am not

Neither I nor the assistant is a mental health professional. Neither of us claims to be one. The assistant is not therapy, not treatment, and not a clinical assessment, and nobody should reach for it as a substitute for one.

There are mental health professionals among the members here. That is one of the things a community made of real people gives you, and it is worth saying so this is not read as a claim that nobody here is qualified. What the assistant offers is different and smaller: an answer at any hour, drawn from what people who have lived this wrote down.

I am saying that plainly and near the top rather than in small print at the bottom, because a tool that talks to frightened people and is vague about what it is has already done something wrong.

What it is an alternative to is narrower than "professional help." It is an alternative to commercial assistants that will not name organized harassment, and to professionals who deny human trafficking outright. That denial is not harmless. A person who is told their account is a delusion, by the service they were sent to for help, comes away with fewer options than they arrived with. Someone whose call comes back to them as a joke from a police officer comes away with fewer still. I believe that path ends in suicides and in mass shootings, and that is why I am building an alternative to it rather than a replacement for care.

Both of those things are true at once: the existing avenue can fail a person badly, and this is not a clinician. Anyone who tells you they have built something that solves both problems at once is selling you something.

Why this is a blog post and not a comment

I wrote all of that as a reply on Quora, underneath my own post.

Quora has erased seven of my accounts, and when a post goes the comments underneath it go with it — mine and everyone else's. Ione's reply would go too, which is somebody else's words disappearing over an account decision that had nothing to do with them.

So it goes here, where it stays, with the exchange captured in full.

Where to find it in the app

- Knowledge Library (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/knowledge) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#knowledge)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/the-assistant-is-not-a-clinician

2026-08-23 · The Record Has Started

Six days ago I wrote that a shared ClickLog entry feeds something that has never existed anywhere: trend data on organized harassment, reported by the people it happens to rather than assembled by outsiders after the fact.

That was a description of a thing that had not happened yet.

What the shared view reads now

The window is the last ninety days. Everything in it falls between August 13 and August 23, 2026.

- Shared incidents: 20
- Tagged incidents: 18
- Members reporting: 2
- Members who logged more than one: 1
- Days with activity: 5
- Countries: 2
- Areas, about 11 km each: 3

Nineteen of the incidents sit in two adjacent areas in Canada, one sits in a single area in the United States. That is the screen, stated the way the screen states it.

The section that a private log cannot produce

Near the bottom of the report there is a list headed "Problem and scheme reported together." It pairs what a member said happened with which named scheme they filed it under, and counts how often the two arrive on the same incident.

Color Sensitization comes back paired with headlights and flashlights shined at you, three times. With cars parked outside your home, three times. With ringing in the ears, twice. With being more tired than you should be, twice.

Being blocked, cut in line or held up arrives paired with six different schemes, one time each — The Altered Ticket, The Engineered Delay, The Replay, Lure to a Location, The Planted Witness, and Color Sensitization again.

Look at what that second one is. The same experience — you are held up — filed six different ways by someone who was there and had to decide what it was. A private log gives you your own six. It cannot tell you that being held up is the joint most-attached problem in the whole set, because it has nothing to compare against.

That list is small and it is one member's tagging. It is also exactly the shape of the thing I have been describing for six days, and it is the first time the shape has had anything in it.

Why you cannot rank the schemes by their counts

Color Sensitization shows five. The Altered Ticket shows one. That does not make one five times more common than the other, and the report says so in its own text rather than leaving a reader to work it out.

Some named schemes run continuously in the background. They can be reported on almost any day, so their counts accumulate. Others are single operations with a start and an end — a ticket gets altered once, a search happens once. Every row in the report prints which kind it is directly under its name.

Which means the honest reading of that section is not a league table. It is a list of what people are naming, with a warning attached about the arithmetic.

Where the two members actually overlap

The report groups problems into six kinds of harm and counts how many different people reported each.

Body and health leads on volume — thirteen incidents — and all thirteen are one member. Two kinds have both members behind them: watched and followed, eight incidents, and threats and intimidation, two.

So the common ground between two people in two countries is being watched and being threatened. Everything else in the set so far is one person's account.

That is a small overlap. It is also the first one that exists at all, and it is the answer to the question underneath almost every conversation survivors have with each other: is this only me.

What twenty entries do not establish

The report answers this itself, in a section printed under the numbers rather than tucked somewhere else. Its own words are that these are first-hand accounts, recorded by the people they happened to and not checked against any outside source, that the people logging chose to join and chose to share, so it is not a sample of any wider population and no rate can be calculated from it.

And that a count is the number of times members reported something, not the number of times it happened. Most incidents are never logged at all.

Two gaps stay open. Nothing in the report says what a member did about an incident or what came of it, so nothing in it shows whether anything helped. And no entry has a second source attached to it.

A number that prints what it cannot carry is worth more than one that does not.

Nothing in that view came out of anyone's notes

Worth repeating whenever a figure gets published, because a published figure is the moment a reader wonders what else went with it.

Your notes are not shown to anyone else here, and they are not in the trend report. The exact location is not in it either — only the area of about 11 km, which is why the report counts areas rather than naming streets. Who logged what is not in it: the view can say two people are behind those entries, never which two. Every figure comes from a grouped query that cannot return any of those things.

Correction, 2026-08-24: this originally said notes are never seen by anyone, including this project. That was wrong; the accurate line is above.

Even the country is not asked. It is worked out inside the app from the approximate area against a table of borders held locally, so nothing about a member is sent anywhere to produce it. That table is a coarse edition, which the report also says: it is enough to tell one town reporting from several countries reporting, and not enough for anything finer.

Sharing is off unless you turn it on, with a per-incident choice on top of the setting. Delete an incident and it leaves every future report. Log privately forever and never share a line, and ClickLog still does its first job completely.

The standing argument

The UN Special Rapporteur on Torture's 2020 report to the Human Rights Council, A/HRC/43/49, addressed coordinated harassment as a category and said the volume of allegations warrants investigation rather than dismissal.

A body that says investigate needs data. Trend data is the form a submission takes, and no individual account has to be adjudicated for an aggregate to be worth looking at.

The data is not mine to keep

The shared totals are published. They are not a private holding this project sits on and rations out.

Anyone who wants them can use them as they like — a survivor checking whether their area appears, a researcher, a journalist, someone drafting a submission. The report prints its own method and its own limits alongside the figures precisely so it can be picked up and used by somebody who was not in the room when it was built.

That is the opposite of how counts of people in our situation have always worked. Every one of them was made by someone standing outside, describing us to a third party, on terms we never saw. This one is made by the people it happens to, and it is theirs.

Where to find it in the app

- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/clicklog-the-record-has-started

2026-08-23 · How to Check Me

Ione (https://www.quora.com/profile/Ione-40) replied to my last post saying they would pass the information on once they did their fact check.

[Screenshot — A post and comment on Quora. Kingmingling writes: Gn0b0dy Pneuma (https://www.quora.com/profile/Gn0b0dy-Pneuma) gave a good post on DEW and shielding. Finding writing like that on Quora is hard, and finding it again later is harder. So, a request. Consider lending your writing to the Knowledge Library. It is a private assistant inside the app, for Targeted Individuals only. You ask it a question and it answers from the writing of people who have lived this. Right now all of that writing is mine — more than 1,500 Quora posts feed every answer it gives. Underneath, in the comments, Ione writes: Not suicidal. If anyone needs help I will pass on this information, once I do my fact check.]

That is the correct instinct and I want to make it easy. Here is how to check me, in order of how little you have to trust me for each one to work.

The blog keeps its own history, and anyone can read it

Every post here is a plain text file, stored somewhere public: https://github.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site

The useful part is that every change to a post is recorded and dated. Not just when it was published — also the day a word in it changed three days later, and which word. Nothing can be altered quietly, because an edit records itself, and that record goes public the moment the edit does.

Posts come in two kinds and they age differently.

Most are fixed once published: arguments, announcements, the progress reports. Their numbers and wording are never touched afterward, because the point of a dated claim is that it shows what was said and when. A post whose figures look out of date is doing exactly what it should.

Two pages are the opposite, kept current on purpose and edited in place — the Dictionary, and the page tracking which of my links are dead. For those, the recorded history is the list of what changed. If you want to know what the Dictionary said last month, it is there.

The app is a public repository too

The application itself is here: https://github.com/chargingthefuture/chargingthefuture

So a claim I make about what the app does is checkable against the thing that does it. When I write that the Knowledge Library keeps contributions in a searched table rather than training a model on them, that is not a promise you have to accept — the code that reads that table is in that repository, and so is the code that removes a row when somebody withdraws.

The same goes for what gets shared. When I write that the ClickLog trend report carries only a date, a rough area and the tags, the code that builds that report is readable, and you can check for yourself what it does and does not put in.

The Wayback Machine has copies I did not make

Every time this blog publishes, an automated job submits pages to the Internet Archive at web.archive.org.

Two things archive: the site's front page, and the raw text file of the post exactly as it sits in the repository. So a copy of the words exists that I did not make and cannot alter, dated by an organization with no relationship to me.

The rendered page does not archive. This blog assembles each post in your browser rather than keeping one as a separate file on the server, so the Internet Archive's crawler asks for something the server does not have. The submission goes out every time and is refused every time. The raw text is the better record anyway — it is what the post says, rather than a picture of it.

To use it, paste the post's raw address into web.archive.org. That address is the repository path with raw.githubusercontent.com in front:

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site/main/wiki-site/content/posts/how-to-check-me.md

Swap the last part for whichever post you want. Then compare the capture against what the file says today.

A step-by-step walkthrough of all of this — finding any post's raw address from GitHub, reading the capture calendar, and comparing an old copy against what is live — is here: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/looking-up-this-blog-in-the-wayback-machine

One honest caveat. That job is best-effort — it is allowed to fail without failing the deploy, because the Internet Archive rate-limits and sometimes refuses a save. So a missing snapshot means the submission did not go through, not that a page was hidden. The captures that exist are real; their absence proves nothing either way.

Correction, 2026-08-24: this section originally said the rendered article page archives alongside the raw text, and told you to paste the post's own address. Both were wrong. The rendered page has never archived successfully, and the raw address above is the one that works.

The feed is numbered, and gaps would show

Every post, in one list, newest first, at https://chargingthefuture.com/feed

Each entry carries a number assigned by publication order, running from the oldest to the newest without a break. That numbering is not decorative — it means a removed post would leave a visible hole, and a backdated post would land out of sequence. The feed also prints which range of how many it is showing, so the total is on the screen rather than something you have to take from me.

What about the Quora posts that no longer exist

Quora has erased seven of my accounts. Everything on them went with them, which means Quora is not a record of anything and cannot be used to check me.

What I do with those posts is restore them into this blog's archive, each one marked with the account it came from, the date it was originally posted, and a link to the original question where one still exists. Those archive entries are frozen at export, which is why an archived post keeps its original spelling and its original title even when they are wrong.

That is a provenance claim, and it is the weakest link in this whole list — you are trusting that the export matches what was posted. Where a question still exists on Quora you can check the entry against it. Where the account is gone, you can check the Wayback Machine for the original address. Where neither exists, you have my word and a file with a date on it, and you should weigh it accordingly.

The posts are drafted with an AI assistant

I dictate and correct; an assistant drafts. The project's disclaimer says the same about the app repository — most development there is done by AI agents: https://github.com/chargingthefuture/chargingthefuture/blob/main/ctf/docs/DISCLAIMER.md

What that means for checking: errors get in that I have not caught yet. When one is found it gets corrected, the correction is dated, and the change sits in the record with everything else. All of this being public is the point, not a claim that nothing is ever wrong.

What none of this proves

A timestamp proves when something was written. It does not make the thing true.

Public code proves what the app does. It proves nothing about whether any account of organized harassment is accurate, mine or anyone else's.

A Wayback capture proves a page existed in that form on that date. It does not prove the page was right.

This matters most where the numbers are. The ClickLog trend report says its own limits under its own figures: the entries are first-hand and unchecked against any outside source, the people logging chose to join and chose to share, so it is not a sample of anything wider and no rate can be calculated from it. That is the report's own text, not my summary of it, and it is there because a figure that hides what it cannot carry is worth less than one that admits it.

So the honest version of what verification gets you here is narrower than proof and more than nothing. You can establish that I said a specific thing on a specific date, that I did not change it later, that the code does what I said it does, and that a third party holds a copy. What happened to me, you cannot check from a repository. Nobody can hand you that.

The short version

Four steps, if you want the method rather than the reasoning:

1. Open https://github.com/chargingthefuture/wiki-site and find the post in the content folder. Its recorded history shows when it was written and everything that changed since.
2. For any claim about the app, read the code at https://github.com/chargingthefuture/chargingthefuture.
3. Paste the post's raw address into web.archive.org — the form shown above — and compare a capture against what the file says now.
4. For anything originally posted to Quora, check the archive entry against the original question, or against a Wayback capture of the address, and treat what neither covers as unverified.

Where to find it in the app

- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)
- Knowledge Library (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/knowledge) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#knowledge)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-to-check-me

2026-08-23 · It Answers From One Person's Writing

The Knowledge Library is a private assistant inside the app — you ask it a question, it writes back an answer drawn from the writing of people who have lived this.

Right now all of that writing is mine. More than 1,500 Quora posts feed every answer it gives.

That is the honest description of it, and it is also the problem with it.

Why one person is the wrong number

One person has one set of circumstances. I travel, so what I know about a staged week is what it looks like when it has to keep resetting. Someone who has been in the same apartment for nine years knows a version of it I have never seen.

One person also has one set of blind spots, and an assistant built on one person's writing inherits every one of them without knowing it has. It will answer confidently about the things I happen to have written about and go quiet, or worse, go vague, on everything else.

Gn0b0dy Pneuma (https://www.quora.com/profile/Gn0b0dy-Pneuma) wrote a post about shielding fabric and faraday cages that contains more practical knowledge on that subject than everything I have ever written about it put together. I could not have produced it. Somebody asking the assistant about shielding deserves that answer, not mine.

Lending, not giving

The word on the screen is lend, and it is meant literally.

You keep every right to your writing. You are not signing it over, you can go on using it anywhere else you like, and you can withdraw it whenever you want — at which point it comes out of the library and the assistant stops drawing on it.

That last promise is the one worth being skeptical about, because plenty of places make it and cannot honestly keep it. So here is the mechanism.

Your words are not used to train a model. Training would blend them into something that cannot be unpicked afterward — once your writing has shaped the weights, there is no row to delete and no honest way to undo it. Instead your posts sit in a table that the assistant searches when it needs them, and quotes from. Withdrawing means the row comes out. After that there is nothing left for it to find.

A tool trained the ordinary way could not tell you that and mean it. This one can, because it was built the other way around specifically so it could.

What it takes, and what it will not use

What is wanted is writing where you describe what happened and what you did about it — practical, specific, from your own life. That is what a person asking for help actually needs.

What will not be used is writing that is only declaration, with no account of what you went through and nothing about how you got through it. That is not a judgment on anyone's faith or on how they make sense of any of this. If you write about faith and about what you did on the ground, that is exactly the kind of post this is for.

[Screenshot — The Knowledge library contribution screen in the Charging The Future app. It opens: The assistant answers from one person's writing. Yours is different, and the next member asking for help deserves both. If your writing is public on Quora about what you have lived through and how you manage it, you can lend that writing here. A section headed How you want to send it offers two choices — Pick a few posts, marked recommended, for pasting the posts that are actually about being targeted, since most people write about all sorts of things and only the author can tell which posts belong; or Send my whole Quora export, better if nearly everything you have written publicly is on this subject, with private messages and drafts stripped out automatically on arrival. A section on getting a copy of your Quora data quotes the Quora Help Center in full and explains in plain words that you email privacy@quora.com or use quora.com/contact and choose I want a copy of my data, that there is no download button in Quora's settings, that the archive usually arrives within 72 hours, and that you send the zip file exactly as Quora sent it without unzipping or cleaning it. A section headed What happens to what you send lists five points: only public answers and posts are kept and everything else in an export is deleted automatically before any person opens it; an uploaded file is never stored, it is read once in memory and discarded; the writing is not used to train a model but goes into a table the assistant searches, which is why withdrawing can actually be done; an accepted contribution earns a ServiceCredits grant, an internal credits unit inside the app that is not money and never cashable, which waits until the member has finished verifying; and contact details are stripped automatically, which narrows what gets through but does not replace the person who reads it. A consent section lists six lines that all must be ticked: that this is the member's own writing published publicly under their own Quora account; that it may be used as reference material the assistant quotes from when drafting answers for members and for nothing else, never sold, published as a dataset, or passed to another company; that the member keeps every right to their writing and is lending it rather than signing it over; that they can withdraw it at any time; that a person will read what they send, that not everything is used, and that no answer reaches a member without a human reviewing it first; and that where their posts name or describe other people, those parts may be cut or the post skipped because those people did not agree to it. An optional field asks whether the writing names or describes anyone else so those parts can be cut. Below that is a form for pasting a link to each post on Quora and the whole text of the post, with a button to add another post and a Send my writing button.]

The six lines

There is a consent section and all six lines have to be ticked before anything can be sent. They are worth reading rather than clicking through, because each one is a limit on what can be done with what you send.

The writing is yours and published publicly under your own account. It is used as reference material the assistant quotes from when it drafts answers for members of this app, and for nothing else — not sold, not published as a dataset, not handed to another company. You keep every right to it. You can withdraw it at any time. A person reads what you send, not everything is used, and no answer reaches a member without a human having reviewed it.

The sixth line is the one most places would not bother with. Where your posts name or describe other people, those parts may be cut or the post skipped entirely, because those people did not agree to any of this. There is a field to tell us who they are so those parts come out before anything is used.

You do not have to be verified first

Contributing to the Knowledge Library is one of a very small number of things in this app that do not wait on being approved through Unlock.

The reason is plain enough: judging a contribution means looking at a real person writing real things, which is the same look verification asks for. Gating it would review the same account twice.

An accepted contribution earns a ServiceCredits grant. ServiceCredits are an internal credits unit inside the app — not money, not cash, never cashable — and members exchange them for things they need. The grant is recognition for building something everyone here uses. It is not a payment for your story, and nobody should read it as one. If you have not finished Unlock, contribute anyway; your writing is read and used exactly the same way, and the grant waits until you are verified.

Why this is worth doing now

Good writing by Targeted Individuals is hard to find on Quora, and harder to find again. It is scattered across accounts, buried under everything else a person writes about, and it disappears without notice — seven of my own accounts have been erased, and everything on them went too.

Lending a post here does not take it off Quora. It puts a durable copy somewhere it can be found by the person who needs it, at the moment they ask, instead of depending on somebody stumbling across it before the account goes.

My own goal for this is statistical zero suicides and mass shootings. That is ambitious and I know it. A private assistant that can answer a frightened person at three in the morning, in the words of people who have been where they are, is one component of getting there.

Right now it can only answer in mine.

Where to find it in the app

- Knowledge Library (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/knowledge) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#knowledge)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)
- Unlock (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/plugin/unlock) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#unlock)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.
