2026-08-19 · If Quora Closed Your Account, There Is Now Somewhere to Say So

When an account is closed, the evidence of it disappears with the account. There is no page left saying what was there, no notice anyone else can read, and nothing you can point at later. The loss is quiet by design, and quiet losses do not add up to anything.

There is now a survey for recording them. It is here, and reading it needs no account at all:

https://app.chargingthefuture.com/survey/quora-account-deletions

What it asks

Two things about you: whether you consider yourself a Targeted Individual, and whether at least one of your accounts was removed. Then one card for each closed account.

Each card asks for the handle, what happened to it, and when. The options for what happened are the things Quora does: the whole account deleted, banned or suspended, posts removed while the account stayed, a Space you ran removed, or blocked from posting. There is no option for closing an account yourself, so nothing in this data is somebody walking away.

It asks what reason Quora gave, and "no reason was given" is one of the choices rather than a blank. That is the answer I expect most often, and it is worth recording as an answer in its own right. It asks whether you appealed and whether anything came back. It asks what the account mostly wrote about, and roughly how many posts and how long it ran, both optional.

Why one card per account

The count of removals is the number of cards, never a number anyone types into a box. That means every removal in any total has a handle and a date behind it, and anyone reading a figure later can ask what it is made of.

It is a smaller number than a typed one would be, and it holds up.

What happens to your handle and your words

Three separate permissions, each one off unless you turn it on:

Your handles may be published. Your words may be quoted. Your handle may be attached to your quote. They are separate on purpose — you can allow your words to be used while staying unnamed, which is the combination most people want.

A response with all three switched off still counts in every total. Nothing is lost by declining, and nothing about you appears anywhere because you filled it in.

What this data can and cannot say

It is self-report. Nothing in it is checked against Quora, and it does not need to be — it is a record of what people say happened to them, which is exactly the record that does not otherwise exist.

It will always be a floor rather than a full picture, for a reason worth naming: only someone who found their way to another platform can answer at all, and only someone willing to make a free account can submit one. So it counts people who kept going and were willing to sign up, and misses everyone who did not. Whatever number it eventually holds, the real one is larger.

It also cannot say what share of Quora, or what share of Targeted Individuals, this represents. It can say how many removals were reported, by how many people, over what period, and I will not stretch it further.

Why submitting asks for a sign-in

Reading takes nothing. Submitting asks you to sign in free, and that is only there to keep bulk junk out of a table meant to be citable. It is not a claim that anyone is verified, and it is not a check on whether you belong here.

Where it is in the app

If you are already a member, the survey sits in the two places you are asked for a Quora profile anyway:

Unlock (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/plugin/unlock) — the verification screens.

Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — the profile edit screen.

If you are not a member, use the link above. It works without an account, which is the point of it.

Get your writing back, then give it somewhere to work

Quora deleted the account. It did not delete your right to a copy of what you wrote, and they will send you one if you ask.

From their own help page (https://help.quora.com/hc/en-us/articles/360000839503-Can-I-get-a-copy-of-my-data), quoted here in case that page moves:

Yes. Quora will send an archive of your content and personal data to your account's primary email address on request. If you would like to request a copy of your data, you may do so by submitting a request via email to privacy@quora.com or by visiting quora.com/contact and selecting "I want a copy of my data" Please note that you will typically receive the archive within 72 hours of our team confirming that we have received your data request.

Once you have that archive, it does not have to sit in a folder. If what you wrote was about being targeted, you can lend it to the Knowledge Library (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/knowledge) — the reference the app's assistant answers from. It means a newcomer asking a question gets an answer shaped by more than one person's experience, and your writing goes on doing work after the platform that hosted it decided otherwise.

Two things then come out of one deletion: a line in a record that can be counted, and writing that is still useful to somebody.

Why any of this

I have lost four accounts to this now, and each time the only record was whatever I happened to write down afterwards. That is not good enough, and it does not have to be how it goes.

Where to find it in the app

- Unlock (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/plugin/unlock) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#unlock)
- Directory (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/directory) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#directory)
- Knowledge Library (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/knowledge) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#knowledge)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-18 · ClickLog: your record, and the record

ClickLog does two jobs with one entry. You log an incident once; what it becomes is up to you.

Job one: your record

Privately, ClickLog is your incident log. Each entry is dated, located if you choose, with notes that are not shown to anyone else here.

Why keep one? Pattern matching. A single incident is noise even to yourself; thirty dated entries are a pattern you can read. Where it happens, when it happens, which schemes repeat. A person who can see the pattern can route around it — different times, different places, fewer of the Specterati in your day.

And the record is the start of justice. Whatever path you ever choose to pursue, it begins with a dated, consistent, contemporaneous record — the kind you cannot reconstruct after the fact, only keep as you go.

Job two: the record

Shared, your entries feed something that has never existed anywhere: real-time, self-reported global trend data on the movement of traffickers and their activities.

Every count of the people in our situation so far has been assembled by outsiders, after the fact, from scattered reports. This is the reverse: the people it happens to, logging it as it happens, onto one map. Where activity clusters. Which schemes run where. How it moves.

Two things follow from that.

The first is avoidance. A live map of where the activity is helps every member steer around it — the same pattern matching as job one, at the scale of all of us.

The second is standing. The UN Special Rapporteur on Torture's 2020 report to the Human Rights Council (A/HRC/43/49) addressed coordinated harassment as a category and said the volume of allegations warrants investigation rather than dismissal. A body that says "investigate" needs data, and trend data is exactly the form a submission takes. Nobody's individual claim has to be adjudicated for the aggregate to be worth looking at — that is what makes trends powerful.

And further out: I believe visible patterns can help reduce suicides and mass shootings. A person who can see their experience on a map of thousands is a person with proof they are not imagining it and not alone — and despair runs on exactly those two beliefs.

The three rules

Privacy is the whole design, and three rules cover it.

One: your notes are not shown to anyone else here, and they are not in the trend report.

Correction, 2026-08-24: this originally said notes are never seen by anyone, including this project. That was wrong; the accurate line is above.

Two: a private incident does not need a location. Add one only if you want it in your own record.

Three: tagging an incident with problems or schemes requires a location and trend sharing, because tags exist to feed the global map. What sharing sends is grouped trend data only — the date, a rough area, and the tags. Never your notes, never your exact location. An incident you keep untagged shares nothing unless you choose otherwise.

Log privately forever and it is still worth it — that is job one. Share when you are ready and every entry makes the map sharper — that is job two. Both run on the same thirty seconds of logging.

The guide walks through it at your own pace: https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log

Where to find it in the app

- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-18 · The DEW List: Naming Every Kind of Attack

DEW stands for directed energy weapon. It is the term survivors use for attacks you feel in your body with nothing visible in the room: heat, pressure, shocks, vibration, forced wakefulness.

I have asked for a list like this before — on Quora, and on the old Discourse forum, where I wrote about documenting DEW attacks globally alongside 24/7 vitals (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/discourse-migrate/collecting-vitals-24-7) and about living with V2K for decades (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/v2k-for-decades). Those platforms are gone or frozen; the asks went with them.

This time is different, because the list has somewhere to live.

What happens with this list

Each attack type below becomes a label in ClickLog — a new label category, alongside the existing problems and schemes. When you log an incident, you will be able to tag it with the kind of DEW attack it was. Tagged incidents feed the global trend map, so the same attack type showing up across cities stops being one person's story and becomes a documented pattern.

The ClickLog guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log) explains how logging works. The short version of the privacy rules: your notes are not shown to anyone else here and are not in the trend report; a private, untagged incident needs no location; tagging requires a location and trend sharing, because tags exist to feed the map.

The working list

The list starts deliberately short: only the attack types I have documented myself in my own posts. Plain names, so a label means the same thing to everyone who uses it.

- V2K (voice to skull) — hearing voices, commentary, or threats transmitted to you alone
- Sleep deprivation — being kept from sleep or jolted awake
- Heart effects — a sudden heart-rate spike or racing heart with no exertion

That is not the whole picture, and I know it. It is the seed.

How to add to it

If you experience a type of attack that is not on this list, comment in the Commons group chat (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) in the app. Describe what it feels like in your own words; a plain name for it gets worked out in the thread. The list grows the way the schemes list grows: from what members actually report, one named entry at a time.

Where to find it in the app

- ClickLog (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/click-log) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#click-log)
- Commons — the group chat (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#commons)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-18 · The Economics That Oppress Us Can Save Us

In an earlier post about classified ads for TIs (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/guides/scoketrelay/classifed-ads-for-tis), I quoted a writer named John Mixon — not a Targeted Individual, writing about American cities generally. He said that in some communities, organized criminals — the evolutionary offspring of gangs — have significant influence on social and economic matters, and that they know their limits and operate within them to avoid crackdowns.

He was describing a component of what I call the Specterati. He just did not have a name for it.

Argue about who; you cannot argue about how

Many will say it is the government. Some will always say that, and I am not here to settle it. What nobody can argue with is the how: the operation runs on economics. Jobs lost on cue. Housing that falls through. Sales that die, rides that never come, prices that change when you show up. Whoever sits at the top, the machine underneath is economic — and it works.

That should change how we respond. You do not out-argue a machine. You build a counter-machine.

The secret society is costume

They tell people they are the Illuminati. It is dressing, worn to create fear.

Look at how it actually recruits. The basis is an evaluation: test whether someone breaks easily. If they fold, fold them in. If they do not, they become a target. That is not initiation into a hidden order — that is churn, at volume. An operation most of a community participates in cannot be a secret society. The secrecy is the story, not the structure.

And the story changes to fit the listener. One survivor is told it is a secret society. Another is told it is God. Another, the government. I was told it is because I act like MLK. Another, that it started when they divorced a narcissist. Different stories, told to keep each of us alone inside our own explanation — while the record shows the same individuals doing the terrorizing.

If economics works for them, it works for us

Here is the part they would rather we not notice: they have already proven the method. Economic pressure, applied consistently by a network, changes lives. They use it to close doors. The same force, pointed the other way, opens them.

That is the case for the Skills Economy — not as a charity, not as an app for its own sake, but as a barrier. Every skill traded inside the community is a door they cannot close. Work found through the Directory, help exchanged through Foundation, a vetted ride, a listing from a trauma-informed host — each one moves a piece of your life out of the machine's reach. Call it economic reform if you like. I call it the obvious move: the thing that oppresses us is economics, so economics is the thing that saves us.

Exit their economy, exit the psyop.

Additional reading

- Joel Montgomery, Past Master of Vandalia Lodge #491, answering "Do people like Freemasons and the Illuminati have control over the world?" on Quora (https://www.quora.com/Do-people-like-Freemasons-and-the-Illuminati-have-control-over-the-world/answer/Joel-Montgomery-2). The view from inside the lodges: no governing council exists, membership is aging and lodges are merging to survive, politics is a forbidden topic at their own meetings, and regimes have persecuted Masons by the tens of thousands — a group with world-shaping power would have protected its own, and could not.

I am referring to this one answer, read in isolation. I do not know the writer and do not vouch for them, their other writing, or anything else on that page.

2026-08-18 · The People Who Backed This Publicly

Before there was much to point at, ten of the earliest supporters put their names next to this thing in public. They commented in favor of the Skills Economy on Quora, on their own pages and under mine, where other targeted people could see it. That is the hardest kind of support to give — public, attached to your name, in a community where being visible costs something.

Those comments are gone. They lived on Quora accounts that Quora deleted, and the deletions took the whole thread with them. Someone arriving today sees a space with no visible history of people vouching for it.

So the record moves here, where nothing gets deleted by a platform decision. Below is each person, what they do, the Member of the Day post that introduced them, and their directory profile. If you are on this list and you want to say it again somewhere it survives, the door is open — and if you would rather not, that is fine too; the profiles are claimable and removable on request.

The profiles

Why make profiles for them? To make it easy to discover survivors to connect with. 2026-08-18 · Skills Economy Phone Wallpapers

[Screenshot — Skills Economy wallpaper — the Stack logo on a dark teal-to-purple background]

The Skills Economy logo — the three ascending bars, for skills built step by step — is now a phone wallpaper. The design keeps the top of the screen clear so your clock stays readable, and the background is dark so notifications stand out.

Where to download

The wallpapers live on the same GitHub releases page as the Android app:

github.com/chargingthefuture/chargingthefuture/releases?q=wallpapers (https://github.com/chargingthefuture/chargingthefuture/releases?q=wallpapers)

Open the newest wallpapers-v release and pick the file that matches your phone:

| File | Fits |
| se-wallpaper-android-1080x2400.png | Most current Android phones |
| se-wallpaper-iphone-16e-1170x2532.png | iPhone 16e (also fits iPhone 13 and 14) |

How to set it

On Android: tap the file to download it, open it from your notifications or Files app, save it to your photos, then long-press your home screen → Wallpaper & style → choose the image from your photos.

On iPhone: tap the file to download it, open it and save it to Photos, then go to Settings → Wallpaper → Add New Wallpaper → Photos and pick it.

If your phone's screen is a different size, either file still works — your phone will scale it to fit. More sizes can be added over time; the design source is in the open-source app repository, so anyone can render a new size.

2026-08-18 · SocketRelay Now Puts Something in Everyone's Hand

A while back I wrote about what replaced the classifieds era, and what Targeted Individuals can use now (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/guides/scoketrelay/classifed-ads-for-tis). The purpose has not changed: a place to post what you need and offer what you have, without interference, among people who understand why the ordinary places stopped working for you.

What has changed is the app around it. That post described version two. We are on version three now — new interface, new address, and one addition that matters more than the redesign.

The gap in the old version

In version two, SocketRelay ran on free and barter. Both are real and both still work. But barter assumes you have something to trade, and being made destitute is the standard outcome of being targeted, not the exception. Jobs go, savings go, belongings go. A survivor with nothing left to trade got the board's generosity and none of its exchange.

What version three adds

Every member who joins receives 100 ServiceCredits on day one. That is stated plainly on the landing page (https://chargingthefuture.com), and it applies to everyone, immediately, with nothing to earn first.

ServiceCredits are the community's internal credits unit — not money, not a currency, never redeemable or withdrawable for fiat value. What they are is a way to exchange inside the network: for food, for goods, for a service you need, for a want as well as a need. Free and barter did not go anywhere. Credits sit alongside them as a third way to move value between survivors.

The practical difference: nobody arrives empty-handed. On your first day here you can respond to someone's post with something real. And when another survivor does something for you, showing appreciation properly no longer depends on whether you happen to own something worth trading.

Where it is

The board is at app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/socket-relay (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/socket-relay). Post what you need. Offer what you have. Everything else about it works the way the original post described.

Where to find it in the app

- SocketRelay (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/socket-relay) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#socket-relay)
- ServiceCredits (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/service-credits) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#service-credits)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-18 · The manifesto, seven months later

I published a manifesto. It said who we are, what we learned, and that I had built a working answer for every one of the 51 problems survivors face. It ends with the line this whole thing runs on: exit their economy, exit the psyop.

It is still here, exactly as written: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/chargingthefuture/The-Answer:-EXIT-THEIR-ECONOMY,-EXIT-THE-PSYOP

I am not going to update it. Not the numbers, not the wording. A page that gets quietly refreshed proves nothing; a dated record proves what was claimed and when. The manifesto is its own dated snapshot, this post is the August one, and the ones that follow will each be their own.

Two numbers, because this is not a typical app

Before the progress, the vocabulary — because these two numbers measure different things and I will use them in every progress post.

Signed up means exactly that: a person created their own account and was approved. 41 people have, against my personal goal of 384.

Recruited is the Workforce number: 97. A recruited person is someone I have researched individually — skills they stated themselves, in their own words, in public — and placed on the skills map with a directory profile the app marks as created by me. Anyone can claim theirs, or tell me to delete it. Nobody has. When I suggested deletion, some people were outraged at the idea — the profile is how other survivors find them.

That gap between 41 and 97 is not people who declined. This community is in trauma. Being engaged here does not always look like signing up for another app, and I refuse to count people the way a typical app counts them. The map records what each person can actually do; signing up records who has walked in the door so far. Both are real. Neither substitutes for the other.

What has moved in seven months

People. In mid-January, 36 people had signed up and there was no skills map. On August 18: 41 approved members, 97 people recruited onto the map, and 453 followers on the Quora space.

The map. In January the idea existed and the instrument did not. On August 18 the app carries the full catalog of the roughly 650 skills a working economy needs, and the 97 recruited cover 158 of them. About a quarter of the list is covered, and each of those 158 came from a real person with a real trade.

The capacity. At Singapore's published output per worker, the 97 people on the map represent $13.8 million a year — what this group holds if every one of them participated. The app computes it live on the Workforce screen, and it tracks the actuals of the signed-up members separately, so potential and actual never get blurred. The derivation is written out in plain language here: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works

The record itself. In January, the writing lived on platforms that could erase it — and they did, three accounts' worth. On August 18 every post lives on this blog with a timestamped history, the feed carries the short version of all of it, and a platform deletion costs one line on one page. The thing you are reading survived precisely because of what got built between January and now.

The shape of these updates

This is the first progress post, and the next ones will look like it: sign-ups against 384, the map, the capacity, what got built. Same shape and same definitions every time, each one dated, so you can put any two side by side and see the direction without trusting anyone's summary — including mine.

The manifesto said what we are exiting and why. These posts are the record of the exit actually happening.

Watch it move any day, any hour, in the Workforce feature. The guide walks you through it at your own pace: https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce

The app is free. If you have a skill, it is one of the 650, and the list has a place for it.

2026-08-18 · It Does Not Matter Who Put You on the List

When it comes to the Specterati, I do not care what they call themselves.

What did the Transatlantic slave traders fashion themselves to be? They had the entire globe participating, in one shape or form. What did Hitler consider himself to be? The label a criminal operation picks for itself has never been the useful fact about it. What it does, at what scale, and who takes part — that is the useful fact.

Who started it matters to a point

Who put you on the hit list is relevant, up to a point. It explains how you got here. Past that point it stops being useful, because of what happens next: once you are on the list, every criminal group in reach joins in, and they run the same schemes (https://www.chargingthefuture.com/schemes) in rotation regardless of who started it.

That is why the stories never match. One survivor is told it is a secret society. Another is told it is the government. Another, a jealous ex, a former employer, a neighbor. Each explanation is true enough to hold that person's attention and keep them working on the wrong question. Meanwhile the schemes are identical across all of us — same rotation, same playbook, different cities. That is not a claim you have to take on faith: the named list is public (https://www.chargingthefuture.com/schemes), built from what survivors actually report.

Build for the part that does not vary

If the identity of the instigator varies and the method does not, then build against the method.

That is the design of the Skills Economy. It never asks who put you on the list. It does not need a verdict, a name, or an agreed-upon theory before it can do anything for you. Work found through the Directory, help exchanged through Foundation, a vetted ride, a listing from a trauma-informed host — none of that depends on knowing who is behind your particular case.

And it works on the one thing none of them are exempt from. They can pick any name they like, tell every one of us a different story, and rotate through as many groups as they want. They still cannot opt out of economics. That is the ground they operate on, and it is the ground we can take back. The last post (https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/economics-oppress-save) makes that case at length.

Skills Economy. Exit their economy, exit the psyop.

2026-08-17 · An addition, not an alternative

A popular account on Quora says the Skills Economy is nonsense. Their position, repeated at volume: keep quiet, keep your head down, all is lost.

I am not going to name the account, because this is not about a person. It is about the two positions, and the disagreement is worth answering once, plainly, because right now these are the only two positions on the board.

First, the credit they are owed

That same account teaches things that are genuinely worth knowing. How not to get falsely committed. How not to fall for the traps that get set for people in our situation.

Keep using those tips. I mean that without irony. Defense is necessary, and someone who teaches it well is doing real work.

Where we part ways

Defense is necessary. Defense is not a plan.

Keep your head down describes today. It says nothing about next year, or the year after, or the ten after that. A person can dodge every trap and still end up alone, broke, and worn down — because dodging traps was the whole strategy, and nothing was ever built underneath it.

I will say the ending plainly, because pretending it is not there helps nobody. What comes after years of keeping your head down, alone and with no income, is despair. Suicide is what despair does quietly. Mass shootings are what it does loudly. Both are what the keep-quiet strategy leads to when it is the only strategy, and I refuse to stop there.

So I am not offering an alternative to defense. Keep every tip. I am offering an addition: income that does not run through people who can be reached. Housing. Rides. People who trade with you instead of watching you.

Defense keeps you alive today. The addition gives you something to be alive for.

"Nonsense" is free

Notice what it costs to call something nonsense: nothing. No risk, no work, no skin. Anyone can say it about anything, which is why it proves nothing.

Trying to solve the problem costs something. Building costs money, hours, and the chance of failing in public. That asymmetry is worth keeping in view whenever you weigh who to listen to — the person who built something checkable, or the person who spent one word on it.

And the Skills Economy is checkable. It is not a mood or a promise. It is math and economics: the catalog of what jobs an economy that size needs, built against Finland, Singapore's public number for what one worker's skills produce, and a live count — 97 people, 158 of 650 skills, $13.8 million in listed capacity — that updates in the app as people join. I walked through every step of it in the last post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/how-the-math-works

You do not have to believe me. That is the entire point of using public numbers.

What is actually nonsense

Human traffickers calling themselves the illuminati — that is nonsense. The costume is the silly part, and arguing about the costume is exactly what it is for.

Strip the costume and what is underneath is old and ugly: children get sorted young. The ones with skills and integrity get marked and trafficked. The ones who submit get folded into the crime. There is no mystery in it and nothing supernatural about it. It is a business, run on recruitment and disposal.

I do not argue with the costume anymore. I look at the business.

They already use this economics

Here is the part that should make you optimistic, strange as that sounds.

The Specterati run on economics. Favors, jobs, housing, rides — handed out to recruit, withheld to punish. Dependency is the whole mechanism. That is an economy, and it works, which is exactly why they use it.

So when someone calls the Skills Economy nonsense, they are calling the other side's own operating system nonsense. The same laws of economics that let them organize people against us let us organize people for each other. The tool is proven. The only question is which direction it points.

The only solution on the board

Survey what is on offer to a survivor right now. Keep your head down. Pray. Wait for an investigation. Post about it. Give up.

Every one of those leaves you alone, and none of them builds anything. As of today, the Skills Economy is the only position on the board that is about solving the problem — and it is the one position that gets called nonsense at volume.

I am fine with that. It means it stands out, and standing out is how the people looking for it find it.

Watch the progress live in the Workforce feature, any hour of any day. The guide walks you through it at your own pace: https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce

The app is free. Keep every defensive tip you have ever learned. Then add something to defend.

2026-08-17 · How the math works

Someone asked me "How?" in a DM this week. I had posted that with 97 people, the network can already point at an economy of $13.8 million a year. They wanted the math.

The answer I sent fits in a few paragraphs, so here it is, cleaned up. Every number in it is public. You do not have to believe me about any of it — you can check each one yourself.

Where 5 million comes from

The United Nations' own reports count about 2 million people describing this. That is only the people who reported. This crime is underreported, and I am the proof: I have never reported mine to anyone, so I am not in that 2 million. The standard assumption for crimes like this is that about half go unreported, so I added 2 million for the people like me.

Then 1 million more for people who do not yet know it is happening to them. I noticed things at age five. I would not have called myself targeted then. It was not until I was nine that I knew for sure, and it got progressively worse from there. There are people today in the years between noticing and knowing.

That makes about 5 million. It is a guess, stated as a guess, and its job is to be replaced: as people join and list what they can do, a real number comes out of the app's data and this one gets thrown out.

Then I found the countries that size

I searched for countries with a population around 5 million that run service-based economies — where most of the work is services, and most of those services do not have to happen in person.

Finland fits. So I cataloged every job and title a working economy that size needs — about 650 different things — and placed the whole list in the app.

Then, every day, I comb Quora for survivors who state their skills, and I map each one against that list. The app labels every profile I create as community-generated and names me as the one who made it. Anyone can claim theirs or have it removed. Nobody has asked to be removed.

Where we are right now

97 people. They cover 158 of the 650 things on the list — 24 percent.

Now the dollar figure. Singapore publishes what one worker in a pure skills economy adds per year: take its total output, divide by its workers, and you land near $150,000. The app runs this calculation live with its calibrated rate of $142,500 per person, which puts our 97 people at $13.8 million a year.

That figure is what our listed skills are worth at the output rate of the best skills economies on earth. A group small enough to fit in one room already holds that much capacity — listed, mapped, and findable. Every day of profile work, every skill added, every request posted moves it.

This is the Workforce screen in the app on August 17, 2026:

[Screenshot — Workforce overview: 5,000,000 population baseline, 2,000,000 workforce at 40 percent participation, 97 recruited, 24 percent skills coverage — 158 of 650 skills — and a $13.8 million GDP potential]

Why Singapore and Estonia

Both were built on skills and nothing else. Singapore at independence had no oil, no farmland, not enough fresh water — its stated strategy was to develop its people, because people were the only asset available. Estonia came out of occupation in 1991 with 1.3 million people, no money, and infrastructure that did not work, and built a functioning modern state largely in software because software was what it could afford.

Both took about two generations.

To say it plainly, because these examples invite a misreading: I am not forming a nation state. No territory, no citizenship, no government, no intention of any. What is borrowed from those countries is arithmetic — proof of what a population holding nothing but its own skills can produce, and how long it takes.

The part that works in our favor

Specterati operators go after skilled and influential people. That is who gets targeted.

Which means the population I am mapping is unusually skilled. I have reviewed the profiles myself, one by one, and the data shows it. The thing that made each of us a target is the same thing that makes this economy possible.

The number that is actually the goal

5 million is the far number. My personal, realistic goal is 384 people working with each other at any given time.

That is enough for the people who no longer want to live, work, or be around Specterati operators. Not everyone will join. Not everyone needs everything the app offers. But 384 people working full time inside our own economy is enough to run one.

384, not 5 million. That is a number a person can watch arrive.

Watch it move, at your own pace

Nothing above asks you to take my word for it, and you do not have to keep up with posts to follow the progress either.

The Workforce feature in the app shows all of this live, 24/7 — the population baseline, the people recruited, the skills covered, the sectors, the gaps. Open it whenever you want and see where things stand: the screen above is what it looks like today, and it changes as people join and list.

If you want to understand the feature at your own pace, the guide walks through it: https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce

And if you have questions after that, chat with me live in the #general channel in the app.

The app is free. If you have a skill, it is one of the 650, and someone here will need it. You do not have to agree with me about who is doing this or why. You have skills. You need work, housing, and rides. So does everyone else here.

Where to find it in the app

- Workforce (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/apps/workforce) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#workforce)
- Commons — the group chat (https://app.chargingthefuture.com) — guide (https://app.chargingthefuture.com/guide#commons)

To sign up: https://chargingthefuture.com. It is free, invite-only, and you can use one part of it and ignore the rest.

2026-08-17 · Our writing has a permanent home

Quora has deleted my account twice. Both times, months of my answers, posts, and conversations disappeared overnight — not because anything was wrong with them, but because that is what platforms do to accounts like mine.

That stops mattering today.

What changed

Everything I write now lives in one place: a public, version-controlled archive at this site. Every post, every screenshot, every product update is a file in a public repository, with a timestamped history of when it was written and how it changed. Nobody can quietly empty it.

This blog is the reading surface for that archive. It now has clear sections:

- Posts — essays and community writing, like this one.
- Product Updates — the weekly record of what changed in the app.
- Guides, Insights, Member of the Day — practical material.
- Archives — the historical record: everything from the closed Discourse forum, and the posts from the erased Quora accounts as they are restored.

What this means for Quora (and any platform)

Platforms are now distribution, not storage. A Quora post from me is a short excerpt and a link back to the full post here. If Quora erases the next account, the links from that account die — and nothing else. The writing, the screenshots, the history of the app and the work: all unaffected, all still here.

The posts from the two erased accounts are being restored to the archive section, each marked with the account it came from, the date it was originally posted, and a link to the original question where one still exists.

Where to follow

Bookmark this site. It is the one address that does not depend on any platform's tolerance: every new post appears here first, and platform posts will only ever point back to it.

Full post: https://chargingthefuture.github.io/chargingthefuture/article/wiki-site/our-writing-has-a-permanent-home

==============================================================================
2026-08-16 · What stays up

Quora deleted every post ever made to my space Skills Economy (https://skillseconomy.quora.com) today, August 16, 2026, as well as banned my personal account: Farah Brunache (https://www.quora.com/profile/Farah-Brunache).

The account is still there. The space is still there. Both are empty. No post history. No traction. Nothing to scroll.

Here is what was in it. Invitations to people to join a skills network. Posts about members trading work, housing, and rides with each other. Arguments against isolating. Arguments against giving up. A standing position that the answer is to live and work together and to stop handing the Specterati your attention.

Here is what I did not do. I did not follow other spaces. I did not post into feeds I had no connection with. Everything went from my own account into my own space, read by people who chose to be there. For a stretch I posted into a void on purpose, so that nothing I was doing could be read as reaching where it was not wanted.

It all went anyway.

An empty room is the more effective version

They did not stop me from posting. I can post again tomorrow.

What they removed is the record, so the space now reads as a place where nothing has ever happened.

Think about what that does to somebody arriving for the first time. They do not see a person who was silenced. They see an empty room. An empty room means nobody is here, so they leave — and they are right to leave, because that is what an empty room means everywhere else in life.

A ban would have worked less well. A ban leaves a mark. People notice a ban and ask what was said in it. An empty space raises no question at all. It just looks like something nobody showed up for.

Now go look at what is still up

There are accounts posting the same text and the same images over and over. Not disguised. Not spaced out. The same block of words, reposted, as far back as you care to scroll.

By any spam policy anyone has ever written, that is spam. It is still there.

What those posts say is that the world is horrible and about to destroy itself. That there is nothing to be done. That it is the government. That it is spiritual warfare. Give up, in one wording or another.

I want to be careful here, because some people saying those things mean them and are having a terrible time. This is not about them. It is about which content gets to stay.

Sort it and the rule is not subtle. Content that leaves a person alone with it stays up. Content that puts people in contact with each other does not.

Spiritual warfare leaves you praying by yourself. Nothing to be done leaves you sitting still. It is the government points you at something with no address, no phone number, and no door. All of it is safe to leave up, because all of it ends the same way: one person, alone, doing nothing.

Live and work together is a different kind of sentence. It ends with people in the same room.

The spam explanation does not survive contact with the spam

There is an ordinary explanation for what happened to me and I am not going to pretend otherwise. I posted invitations over and over for months, each one carrying a link to the same outside site. Repetition plus an outside link is what automated filters are built to catch.

That explanation has a problem it cannot get past. If a filter were catching repetition, it would have caught the accounts posting identical text and identical images on a loop, in the open, for months. Those are untouched. Mine is gone.

So either a filter is not what happened to me, or the filter runs in one direction. I cannot tell you which from where I sit and I am not going to claim I can.

There is even a version where nobody intended any of it. A system tuned to spot organized outreach looks for repetition, outside links, and named recipients. That is exactly the shape of an invitation. A wall of identical text saying the world is ending carries no link and asks nothing of anybody, so it trips nothing at all.

If that is what happened, it is worse rather than better. A person can be found and stopped. A filter that quietly clears out the connecting content and leaves the isolating content just keeps running, and there is nobody to catch at it.

Either way the result is the same and you can check it yourself in five minutes. Open spam telling people to give up is still there. A space telling people to work together is empty.

What I actually take from it

Not that Quora is against me. That a platform is a lever.

Everything I have written says the same thing about employers, landlords, and anybody else your life runs through. If a person or a company can be reached, they are a lever, and the fix is not to appeal to them. The fix is to need them less.

This is exactly why the app exists and why none of it sits on top of a platform. Accounts get deleted. That is a known thing that happens to people in this situation, which is why the app is the fallback and not the other way around. Your profile, your skills, and the people who can reach you do not live on somebody else’s shelf.

Nothing about the network changed on August 16. The profiles are still there. The skills are still listed. The requests are still open, and there are still more people asking than answering.

If you are reading this, you found it some other way. That is the entire point.
